Chapter 9
THE MAY DEMONSTRATIONS
The Zenkyo did not limit its activities solely to
spheres which attracted the interest of teachers. In
anticipation of the first free May Day since the War, the
Zenkyo arranged a mass meeting in order to draw plans for
its participation in the 1946 May Day demonstration.
In addition, the meeting considered other problems
and grievances. Committees representing three schools came
to the meeting to enlist the aid of the Zenkyo in securing
the discharge of an unpopular principal and the rehiring
of discharged teachers.1 The Zenkyo agreed to lead a dele-
gation to the Ministry of Education to interview the Minis-
ter. The delegates were met by the vice-minister, who was
disinclined to enter into a discuseion. Some of the dele-
gates, unrestrained in their speech, forced themselves into
the vice-minister's office and demanded that May Day be de-
clared a holiday and that the Ministry of Education recog-
nize the right of the teachers to control the schools in
adcition to presenting the demands for the reinstatement
of discharged teachers.2 The vice-minister rejected the
demands. This occasioned another outburst against the
This was the first of a series of unconventional meet-
ings between the Zenkyo and officials of the Ministry of
Education. Throughout the month of May there were a number
of incidents which illustrated the hostility between the
Zenkyo and the Ministry of Education.
Rerorts of the May Day demonstration indicate to what
Gegree the Zenkyo had identified itself with other labor
unions. Zenkyo members joined the parade, bearing red flags
and placards demanding living wages and popular control of
education. Prominent Zenkyo leaders participated; they

delivered speeches urging teachers and workers to unite.4
Children paraded with red flags despite the Ministry's dis-
approval of celebrating May Day as a holiday.5
The continuing Zenkyo demonstrations accentuated the
union's campaign to realize its demands. Zenkyo delegations
conducted "demonstration parades to the Ministry of Education,
sent dozens of representatives, sometimes even hundreds of
them, who foroed their way into the Ministry of Education and
persisted in demanding an interview with the Minister, and
even started a 'sit-down' strike in the Ministry of Education
for several days and nights."6
On 4 May, a Zenkyo committee was granted an interview
by Minister of Education ABE. The Zenkyo made two economio.
demands: the one was for ysoo base salary; the other was the
distribution of food.
The Minister replied that the proposed budget for educa-
tion had been drastically cut by the Government and that he
could do no more than to investigate the food situation.
The Zenkyo restated its political demands.
The Minister refused to consider the Zenkyo plea for
school control and denied the right of any other agency to
control education.
The Zenkyo urged the Minister not to interfere with the
activities of the union.
The Minister declared that he would interfere with the
unions whenever the situation warranted such action.? The
purpose of a labor union, contended the Minister, was to
improve labor and economic conditions. "Unions which aim
at political or social movements are not recognized as real
unions....If their activities should expand beyond the limit"
of legitimate labor union interests, the Ministry would not
be indifferent.8

THE MAY DEMONSTRATIONS
The Zenkyo delegates so persistently pureued their
course of seeking interviews that the Minister was obliged
to hide himself for a few days.9 The Zenkyo delegates, how
ever, were determined to force the Ministry to meet their
demands. They spent a night in the Ministry and invited
committees from the prefectures to come to the Ministry to
unite with them against the Minister. The newly-arrived
prefectural committees joined with the Tokyo delegation and
called on Mr. Kotaro TAnakA, the Ministry official who was
to become the Minister of Education within a few days.
Mr. TANAKA described the attitude of the Zenkyo leaders,
Masanobu INAGAKI and Masao IWAMA, as "simply outrageous,"
and charged, "INAGAkI read forth their resolution about teach-
ers' pay increase with a volley of the most arrogant and in-
sulting words absolutely beneath the dignity of educatore,
and denounced the Education Ministry, the Cabinet, and the
Emperor....He did not Mister me, and even neglected the use
of such common honorifics as daily social intercourse re-
quires...Even at that stage it was quite obvious that they
had a political objective in mind and intended to expand
their influence."10
Nothing was accomplished. Some Zenkyo delegates spent
another night in the Ministry building, and the next day
they expressed opposition to Mr. TANAKA succeeding Mr. ABE
as Minister of Education.ll Immediately after his assumption
of office, Mr. TANAKA received delegates from the Zenkyo and
from the Federation and stated that he would try to improve
12
the teachers' welfare without considering the zenkyo demands.
Dissatisfied, the Zenkyo and Federation delegates returned on
25 May to consult with the Minister. The Federation dele-
gates began to confer with the Minister by themselves. When
the Zenkyo committee arrived, it asserted that the Zenkyo
was the representative of the teachers. The dispute was
futile.
The Minister again gave neither side any encourage-
ment.13

Chapter 10
ATTEMPTS AT UNIFICATION
In May, notwithstandin, their political differences,
the Zenkyo and the Federation conducted negotiations to
consolidate.1 A unified teachers' movemont in Tokyo
probaoly would serve to unite. the independent prefeotural
unions under Tokyo leadership. Thus, a centrally-directed
teachers' organization embracing the whole country gonsid-
erably would enhance the bargaining power and political
pressure of the union.
The basic iccologleal differences between the redicals
and the conservativce, however, obstructed agreement. One
of the primary points of differenee was the attitude to-
ward political parties: the Zenkyo asserted that unions
would be ineffective unless they functioned politically,
while the Federation reaffirmed its adnercnce to political
neutrality. The Federation voiced its objecsion to parades
and demonstrations, indicating that such tegtics redounded
to the discredit of the teacherst movement.?
To circumvent the obstacles to conciliation, both
unions agreed to avoid controversial matters at a joint
meeting of their delegates scheduled for 1 June 1946.
The final sessions of the committoes to arrange the
grand mass meeting ended in disagreement on the salary
question. The Zenkyo sought ¥6oo base pay to which al-
lowances and increments would be added; the Federation
demand was limited to a minimum net income of vs0o.3
The Federation restated its objections to the participa-
tion of parents and children in union demonstrations as
well as its opposition to political action.

On 1 June 1946 delegates from the Tokyo and prefeotural
unions assembled in the Imperial Plaza to institute a united
teachers' organization. To the discomfiture of the Federe-
tion delegates, a group of adults and children came bearing
placards with Zenkyo slogans which urged aid for teachers
end food for children.4 Reports were delivered on the pro-
gress of the unions, and demands for presentation to the
Ministry of Education were drawn.5 The meeting was disrupted
when NOzakA, a leading Japanese Communist, rose to deliver a
speech pledging the support of his party to the teachers.
The Federation delegates as well as .mombers of other conser-
vative unions voiced protests and their opposition to the
Communists. NOzAkA resumed, and the Federation delegates left
the meeting in a body and held an assembly of their own.6 They
regarded the appearances of the children with placards and of
NOZAkA as violations of the Zenkyo promise to avoic controver-
sial issues.
The Federation at its own assembly immediately disasso-
ciated itself from the mass meeting being held in the Plaza
and dispatched a message to the Ministry of Education indicat-
ing that the delegates of the mass meeting were not to be rec-
ognized as representatives of an all-union conference.7 When
the delegates of the mass meeting arrived, at the Ministry with
their demands, the Minister refused to see then. The Zenkyo
attributed the failure of the mass meetin: to the separatist
tactics of the Federation, which was "too prejudiced ageinst
the Communists".8 The Federation countered with the reply
that teachers ought to realize their aims through their own
efforts, without resorting to politics.

Chapter 11
NATIONAL UNIONS ANT THEIR SUBSIDIARIES
The failure of the Federation and the Zenkyo to unite
stimulated each of these unions to recruit strength through-
out the country. Each group sought to consolidate the unaf-
filiated prefectural unions under its leadership. While
the Zenkyo had indicated some successss in setting up nuclei
in the important cities, the Federation thus far had made no
effort to expant nationally. The publicity attendant upon
the attempts of the Tokyo teacherst union to win concessions
from the Ministry had created interested in the prefeotures.
Prefectural delegates nttended union meetings and on occa-
sion participated in demonstrations. The Federation deter-
mined to bring the scattered prefectural unions under its
control.
Shortly after the 1 June meeting, the Federation, col-
laborating with the independent teachers' unions in the
Tokyo area, announced plans to federate those teachers of
Japan who favored a moderate course of action.I Invita-
tions were issued by the Federation to all unions in the
country to attend a conference to establish e national
conservative union. The Zenkyo expressed interest in at-
tending the conference, but the Federetion, distrustful
of the zenkyg, refused admittance to delegates of the
rival union.2 On. 21 June 1946, the conference, limitec to
nonradical unions, successfully combined a number of pre-
fectural as well as city unions in a new national organiza-
tion called the Kyoin Kumiai Zenkoku Renmei (National League
of Teachers' Unions), 3 or the Kyo Zen Ren.

The newly-organized League renewed the demands which the
Federation had made. In the main they were economic. De-
mand for the immediate rant of a ¥loo allowance for each mem-
ber of a teacher's family was coupled-with the demand for sal-
ary increases. The demands also incorporated a plea for the
abolition of discrimination in the salaries of women teachers.
The League also urged relief for undernourished children, and
free distribution of textbooks and school supplies. A committee
presented the Minister of Education with the demands. Although
the League protested its nonpolitical character, its committee
visited Daet members of the various rolitical parties,. including
the Communists, to enlist their support.4
The Zenkyo, following a similar course, called a national
conference a few days later with the objective of attracting
prefectural unions. Prefectural unions were cognizant that
the Zenkyo had become closely associated with the Communists
in the public mind, and consequently, they were reluctant to
affiliate with a radical organization.5 Some prefectural
unions suggested that their objection to joining the Zenkyo
could be overcome by changing the name. The Zenkyo, acting
on this suggestion, dissolved itself, and reorganized under
a new name, the Nihon Kyoin Rodo Kumiai (Japan Educational
Labor Union), or Nikkyo Ro, on 26 June 1946.6. There is no
evidence that a change occurred in leadership or in principles.
While its membership may have increased by this move, the degree
of its expansion is not known.?
By the end of June, the differences between the redical
and conservative unions apparently were more clearly delineated.
The successor to the Zenkyo, the Nikkyo Ro, found itsslf faced
with a united front of conservative unions led by the National
League of Teachers' Unions. The struggle for the controi of
tho toachors' union movoment had expanded from a city to a
nation-wide scalo in a few months.

sumed a course of action calculatcd to draw public participe-

Chapter 12
EFFORTS TO OBTAIN HIGHER SALARY SCALE
THE ANTI-STRIKE BILL
In July 1946, the Ministry of Education challenged the
teachers' unions. The Labor Relations Adjustment Bill, which
was to outlaw strikes on the rart of civil servants such as
police and firemen, was drawn up for presentation to the Diet.
The Minister of Education successfully urged that teachers be
included in the provisions of this measure.1 The radical and
conservative unions imnediately declared their opposition to
a law which would derrive them of the right to strike. The
Conservative League protested to the Ministry of Education and
to the Dict and requested that the provisions of the bill re-
stricting teachers be rescinded. The League contended that a
teacherst union could not "be compared with those of the po-
licemen, the firemen, and the jailors, who work for the pre-
servation of public order".2 The rassage of this bill, pro-
tested the League, would reduce the unions to the status of
"cultural and social institutes".3 Leaguc continued its at-
tack, expressing a lack of confidence in the overnment for
its dilatory tactics with regard to the demands of the teach-
ers.4
The Nikkyo Ro, equally determined in its denunciation,
interpreted thc bill not only as a government conspiracy
against teachors, but as an attempt to "surpress all dis-
putes"5 and "to put education again under the despotic control
of the bureaucrats".6
The Government responded to the rotgsts and excluded
teachers from the rrovisions of the bill.

PROGRAMS OF THE NATIONAL UNIONS
Shortly after the defeat of the antistrike bill, the
national unions joined again in opposition to the govern-
ment on the salary question. The government had granted
s ccial allowances in February, Arril, and June to avert
distress, but it dic not reform the salary schedule un-
til Sertember 1946.8 The new pay system merely revised
the method of deternining salarics, rather than raising
them, although a slight increase was included for low-raid
elementary and youth school teachers.9 In general, the de-
mands of the unions were ignored.
In October the League called a conference to renew the
camraign. New demands were adorted. The first of these
urged that a ¥6oo net salary be established until a jerma-
nent base ray could be fixe. The conference also recom-
mended that income tax exemrtions be raised, that discrim-
ination against women teachers be abolished, and that allow-
ances be increased. The League also declared its ori osition

The Zen Kyo Kyo, collaborating with other labor unions,
made final preparations to declare a general strike on 1
February 1947. At the very last moment, however, a SCAP
order which pointed out the risks to Japanese economy im-
plied in a general work stoppage, forbade the strike.65
With the issuance of the SCAP order, the campaign com-
Kyo notified its prefectural branches that the campaign
against the inistry wguld continue despite the suspension
of the general strike.65 The conservative League raised its
demands. Resuming its campaign on 5 February, the League
submitted to new Minister of Education TAKAHiSHI a proposal
that the base pay be increased by 25 percent and that a uni-
form ¥3oo raise be given. In addition to regular allowances,
the League further suggested a ¥2o0 allowance for each de-
pendent of teachers living in one of the six big cities and
¥150 for the inhabitants of other localities.S6 The Minis-
try was not prepared to act favorably on these demands.
Within a few days, a Zen Kyo Kyo delegation, at an inter-
view with the Minister, reiterated its demands for the es-
tablishment of a higher base pay and for greater allowances.
On 13 February the Government announced a temporary
salary scale which would provide a loo percent inerease in
the net pay of teachers earning M600 or less. 68 The Govern-
ment promised to discuss the fixing of a permanent salary
scale within a month.9 The unions acceptod.
The Zen Kyo Kyo pressed for further concessions. t
the second interview with the winister, collective bargain-
ing was discussed, and on 8 March the Ministry of Education
and
the Zen Kyo Kyo signed a contract which estab-

Livelihood of Teachers
The following figures were given orally by one of the competent
treasurers of the ducation Ministry; so they cannot contain any big
errors, if they may not be mathematically exact. All of the figures
refer to the period between April 1945 and March 1946, and they are
not take-home salaries, but legal original ones. The amount of the
bonus indicated below includes that part of the bonus which will be.
distributed at the end of March 1946 and which is exceptionally high
compared with the past.
The expenditures vary of course widely, depending on each indi-
vidual case, but we try to give a fair average.. The figures given
below in regard to expenditures are partly based on private conversa-
tions with teachers of various schools.

already spent all their savings. One of my relatives, who is also a
university lecturer, is living on the kimonos of his wife, which he is
selling at black market price." The Ministry of ducation has also
reports of primary school teachers who are existing only on.black-.
marketing.

The Colloctive Bargaining Contract
Betwe.n he Council of All-Jopan Tonchers' Unions
and the Ministry of Education
The Ministry of Education. heroinafter referred to as A, and
Zen Nihon Kyoin Kumiai Kyogi Kai (The Council of All-Japan Teachers'
(Uaions). hereinafter referrod to ns B, hereby make an agroomont for
colloctive bargaining based upon the spirit of tho Labor Union Law, as
follows:
Chapter I. Principlos
Article I
A shoall recognize B es an agent for colleotive barg ining. A shall
be rosponsible for the security of the living of the members of B.
A and B shall cooperate and be responsible for the enhancemont of
democretic education.
Article II
A shall not make any discrimination based on sex di not:ion'against
the members of B.
Chapter II. Matters Concerning Fay
Article III
A shall undertake to establish a pay system that will seoure for
the members of B and their families the standards of wholesome
ond cultured living. In deciding fund mental principles of pay,
the representatives of both perties shall participate.
Article IV
A shall confer with D to establish a retirement bonus system without
imposing a burden on the members of B.

Chapter III. Matters Concerning Working Hours
and Business Affairs
Article V.
The working hours of teachers shall be as follows!
1. There shall be 42 working hours  week.
2. The standard daily teaching time shall'be
4 hours.
3. As to other working hours, A and B shall de-
cide by mutual agreement within the limits
of preceding rules.
Article VI
Twenty free study days shall be granted every year.
Chapter IV. Matters Concerning Free Days
and Vecstions
Article VII
Sundays, holidays, end Labor Day shall be free days.
Employees shell be paid for free days.
Article VIII
Twenty free days each year shall be granted in consideration
of service.
Article IX
Three physiologicel free days in a month and 16 weeks
off duty before and after childbirth shall bo grantod.
Absenoc duo to prognancy sheil bo recognizod as froe
days upon presentation of medical certification.
For ono year aftor childbirth, nursing hours shall be
grented.
Chaptor V.
Matters Conccining Sicknoss
and Modical Trea'ment
artiole X
Throo yoors with full pay shall be allowed for the
treatmont of tuberculosis.

 trip on union business shall be treated ns an officiel trip after

AppendixH
Memorandum
A promises the fulfillment of the following as essential to the
establishment of democratic education and the maintenance and im-
provement of working conditions.
1. The number of teachers in one school shell be
large enough to assign at least three to each
two classes. In case there is a fixed official
number of teachers which exceeds the number here
prescribed. the number of teachers sholl be at
lesst the officially fixed number or more.
2.
The number of students in each class shall be
less than 40.
3.
Tuberculosis shall be regarded as sickness con-
tracted while on duty.
4.
A shall establish more han one sanatorium for
tuberculosis patients in each prefecture without
delay, and treatment shall be free.
5.
For the protection of female teachers, measures
to provide for ihe expenses of cnfinement shall
be taken.
6.
A persen on suspended service shall be paid in
full.

=== Page 51 === (confidence 0.99)
Appendix M
Excerpt from Ministry of Education
Hatsu Gaku No. 1er, 5 April 1946,
published by Bureau of School Education.
We hear that conseguent to the worsening of the food situation,
some teachers and officials of schools have recently been demanding
foodstuffs from the pupils or their parents. It is a matter of deep
regret, since it will become a menace to the home life of the chil-
dren who are under the same ration system as everyone else, including
teachers. You are therefore requested to take the measures necessary
immediately to prohibit strictly such i licit activities on the part
of teachers and officials of the schools.
Appendix N
Excerpt from Ministry of Education
Hatsu Gaku No. 187, 11 April 1946,
published by Bureau of School Education.
Everyone recognizes that the measures taken by the Government
cannot keep pace with the problems which arise from the difficulties
in our daily lives, caused by the shortage of commodities, inflation,
etc. It is therefore necessary that attempts be made by teachers to
help themeolvee It is desirable for them to organize some sort of
self-aid organization. They must be careful, however, not to be too.
radical, or to allow themselves to be taken advantage of by any
political party. It is hoped that these will be developed into a
beneficial organization.
Therefore you are requested not to impose too many restrictions
on such groups.
Teachers are at liberty to identify themselves with any political
party according to their choice, but they must be very careful not to
forget their fundamental mission of education and not to cause any
conflict in the schools, thereby disrupting the education of young
people.
We understand that there are many school farms and other various
organizations supported by parents which contribute toward the living
of elementary aa sec ondary school teachers on the part of parents.

:Subject: Disciplinary Action in Labor Disputes.

E
c
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to enclose a copy of a memorandum dated
May 20, 1948 prepared by the Labor Division of the Economic and
Scientific Section of this Headquarters concerning disciplinary
action by both Occupation and Japanese courts in labor disputes.
Complaints that excessive penalties have been imposed upon labor
union members involved in strikes and other disputes are being
made in Japan with increasing frequency, particularly by left-
wing groups and union members. The enclosed memorandum, which
was prepared at my request in response to an inquiry by the
British Commonwealth Member of the Allied Council for Japan, dis-
2
cusses allegations made in a recent petition (copy enclosed) to
the Supreme Comander for the Allied Powers by the Liaison
Council of All Trade Unions of Japan.
The memorandum concl udes that in cases involving the author-
ity of the Occupation, provost courts are justified in imposing
penalties in accordance with directives of this Headquarters;
and that, in cases tried before Japanese courts, it lies within
the power of the Japane se people to remedy through democratic
machinery any penalties thought excessive. It is the position
of the Labor Division that, if the Japanese people are secure in
CS
their basic democratic freedoms, it is their responsibility and
ANV
not that of the Supreme Commander to remedy any situation in
which the Government is alleged to have transgressed labor rights.
By implication it would appear that the penalties in the cases
described are not considered by the Labor Division to be so
excessive asto infringe the civil liberties of the Japanese
involved.
Respectfully yours,
894.5043
Enclosures:7.
W. J. Sebald

=== Page 75 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to Despatch No. 317 dated June 1, 1948 from the
Office of the United States Political Adviser at Tokyo, on the
subject of: "Disciplinary Action in Labor Disputes."
Economic and Scientific Section
Labor Division
JSK/SR/mem
20 May 1948
MEMORANDUM FOR: Mr. Sebald, DS, GHQ, SCAP.
SUBJECT:
Disciplinary Action in Labor Disputes
1. Recent major cases involving disciplinary action in labor
disputes include:
a. During the course of a strike by the Japan All-Commu-
nication Workers' Union in Tokyo-to 26 March 1948, the union cut
all telegraphic circuit s except those leased for the exclusive
use of the Allied Forces. Among circuits cut were those used by
the Mainichi newspaper which also serviced BCON. Therefore, local
union leaders were instructed by a Signal Corps officer to re-
instate the se lines. Upon their refusal to do so, YAMANOUCHI
Toshio, the local union's strike committee chairman, and NOZAWA
Shozaburo and SHINOZAKI Mitsuji, both members of the national
union's strike committee, were arrested and brought before a
provost c∞urt, charged with refusal to obey an order from an officer
of the Occupation. A First Cavalry Divi sion Provost Court found
YAMANOUCHI and SHINOZAKI guilty on 15 April; NOZAWA was acquitted.
YAMANOUCHI was sent enced to three years at hard labor and fined
*75,000; SHINOZAKI was sentenced to one year and fined ¥50,000.
b. Under Article 38 of the Labor Relations Adjustment Law,
administrative workers of the national government are not allowed
the right of acts of dispute. However, the All-Japan Finance
Workers' Union (i.e., the government tax collectors) resorted to
acts of dispute, such as mass absenteeism, for a period of several
months. After repeated warnings, the Ministry of Finance on 29
March 1948 discharged six national union leaders and fined 24
other leaders 10 percent of their salary for six months.
c. An act of dispute peculiar to Japan has been use of
"production control," a form of dispute by which the union, instead
of striking and forcing a cessation of production, takes over the
plant's production for the course of the dispute. There has been
considerable disagreement within Japanese circles as to the legali-
ty of this form as a proper act of dispute; however this Headquarters
has held that a solution to this question poses a problem for the
Japanese people and that GHQ, SCAP, will not influence a decision.
Normal procedures would leave such a decision up to the Japanese
law courts; however in two cases where Japanese courts did act,
their authority was defied by unionists involved and police action
resulted. These were:
(1) In the case of the Aikodo Printing Company, Tokyo,
where production control has been in effect since
October

=== Page 76 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to Tokyo's
No. 317 dated June 1, 1948.
-2-
October 1947, the Attorney-General's Office and the
Tokyo-to Procurator's Office took action against the
Printing and Publishing Workers' Union, charging
that the union had violated laws protecting the right
to do business and protecting property against tres-
pass. Nine union leaders were arested on these
charges and the case now is being tried in criminal
courts. In the meantime, the nine have been released
on bail and production control is continuing at the
plant.
(2)
In the case of the Nippon Typewriter Company, produc-
tion control has been in effect in four of the com-
pany's plants, Hatagaya, Mita, Chofu (all in Tokyo-to)
and Maebashi (Gumma Prefecture) since November 1947,
as a result of a dispute with the All-Japan Machine
& Tool Workers' union concerning wages and reduction
in personnel. At the Maebashi plant, the Gumma pre-
fectural court on 5 March issued an order placing the
property in custody of the court pending judicial
decision; no violence resulted from enforcement of
this order. In the other three plants, similar
orders were issued by local courts on 21 and 22 April;
a peaceful disposition was made at the Chofu plant
but violence between the police and unionists resulted
at the Hatagaya and Mita factories. Although there
were many arrests, all have been released except six
at Mita who are charged with interference with police,
etc.
There have been other cases where the Japanese courts have issued
provisional attachments in cases of production control, such as the
Riken Company plant in Nagaoka, but there has been no interference
with court officials pending legal disposition.
2. It is not believed that in any of the instances mentioned
above has the action been of such a nature as to threaten seriously
labor's rights as outlined in the FEC 16 principles (FEC-045/5, 6
December 1946).
a. In the cases involving occupation authority, it has
been the position of this Headquarters that strikes must not inter-
fere with Occupation purposes; in that case, the provost court
certainly had the fullest legal justification for its action.
b. The case involving the tax collectors essentially
poses a question of the government's authority to discipline its
employees in an instance where the employees, despite repeated warn-
ings, disobeyed the Japanese law. If the government acted unwisely,
the remedy lies in the power of the Japanese people to elect a new
government or, through the Diet, to impose another decision.
c. In

=== Page 77 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to Tokyo's
No. 317 dated June 1, 1948.
-3 -
c. In the same manner, the Japanese people, through
normal democratic channels, have the power to redress any griev-
ance of the various unions in the production control cases al-
though, it might be pointed out, no decisive court decision has
yet been issued. There seems to be no question, however, of the
legal or moral right of the government to enforce a court order
against the wishes of the persons affected.
3. In essence, the above cases, excluding that which was dis-
posed of by the provost court, represent a natural attempt by
either the government (as employer) or private employers to exer
cise rights which they believe they have in existing Japanese law.
To the degree that this represents establishment of a balance in
labor relations it is considered to be a healthy development.
4. Generally, it is the position of this Division that if
the Japanese people are secure in their basic democratic freedoms,
it is their responsibility--and not that of SCAP--to remedy any
situation wherein the government is alleged to have transgressed
on labor rights.
/s/
JAMES S. KILLEN
Chief, Labor Division

Enclosure No. 2 to Despatch No. 317 dated June 1, 1948 from the
Office of the United States Political Adviser at Tokyo, on the
subject of: "Disciplinary Action in Labor Disputes."
SUPPLICATION
27 Apri1 1948.
TO:
General Douglas Macarthur, SCAP
FROM:
Liaison Council of All Trade Unions of Japan
Dear General Douglas MacArthur:
Your wise and sublime policies here in Japan which have contri-
buted so much to the democratization of Japan, are attracting world
wide attention. By repeated instructions of General Headquarters of
Allied Forces, all the Japanese are now aware that the development
of sound and free labour unions is essential to the policy of democra-
tization. However, it is a matter of regret that in the recent dis-
pute of All Government Employees, and other struggles there were so
many who were punished or were illegally kept in confinement. Upon
some of the members of the All Japan Communications Workers Union,
five years' penalty of heavy labour and the sum of seventy-five thou-
sand yen (¥75,000) were imposed; likewise members of A1l Financial
Department Workers Union were dismissed from their posts; or among
the members of All Printers and Publishers Union Aikodo and All Japan
Machine Tool and Typewriter Manufacture Workers Union, there are a
great many prisoners. These facts we believe are checking the free
and sound development of Japan's labour unions and are a great
obstacle to the democratization of Japan.
In the name of five million (5,O00,O00) workers in the Liaison
Council of All Trade Unions of Japan, we here beseech you that by
your wise jud gment those who are under sentence should be acquitted
or have their sentences lightened, while for those illegally arrested
the order of their release should be given.

調查結果の概況
(T) 單位勞働組合の部
1. 組合數及組合員數
昭和 22年12月末現在の組合總数は28,013組合、組合日數は 6,268,432人で、同年六月末現
在と比較すると、組合数は4,691 組合、組合員数は 673,733 人の增加である。增加率は組合數
に於ては20%、組合日数に於ては12%である。一組合當り平均組合員数は224人で、六月末
の平均組合日数 240人より16人の減少を示してみる。即ち組合数の方が組合員数の増加率よ
り大なること及び平均組合日数の減少は、組合組織が中小事業所の勞働者に擴大されてること
を質證してる。
2. 産業别分布
組合数の産業別分布は、製造工業が第一位で11,466組合（組合總数の40.9%）次いで運輸通信
業 4.246 組合(15.2%)公務及周體 4,093 組合(14.6%) 自由業2,471 組合(8.8%) 建設工業 1,332
組合（4.8%）辦業1.116組合（4.0%）が主なるものである。
組合1日数は製造工業2,117,616人を等要に（總組合日数）33.8%）運輸通信業1,337,829人（21.3
%）山業 552,357人(8.8%）業 545,598人（8.79%）公務及團體 529,931人(8.5%）建設
工業395,521人（6.3%）が主要なるものである。
3. 府縣别分布
組合数の府縣別分布は東京2,946 組合（組合總数の 10.5%）を第一位として、北海道2,186組
合（7.8%）の兩地區が群左投き、次で大队1,539組合(5.5%）福同1,009.組合(3.6%）兵庫
1,007組合(3.6%）受知930組合（3.3%）融余用862組合（3.1%）以上が800以上の組合が
結成されてる都道府縣である。組合日数の府縣別分布の第一位は矢張り東京で804,764人（組
合日總数の 12.89%）次至司 458.277 人(7.2%)大版445,628人(7.1%) 北海道 391,695人
(6.2%)受知 329,115人(5.3%)兵月 289,899人(4.6%)神奈用 271,837人(4.3%)が 25萬
人以上の組合日を擁する都道府縣である。
4. 性别分布状况
組織勞働者の男女の制合は略々男子77に對し女子23である。女子の占める制合を産業大分類
別に觀ると、金融業42.36、自山業38.5%、サ-ピス業36.3%、製造工業29.0%、商業23.3%、
公務及團體 23.1%、典業 19.5%、運检通信業 15.4%、その他の產業 14.3%、業 13.7%、方
ス電氣水道業9.9%、林業9.8%、水産業7.5%、建設工業4.9%の順である。更に製造工業の
紡織工業は72.8％で羊である。卽ち産業別の男女の制合と略を照應してる。
5. 事業所続計調査と組合日數との比較
昭和22年10月總理計局で施行した事業所統計調査のうち「常雇の職日及び勞務者数」と
本調査の勞便組合日数とがどのような比率になるかを求めると67％である。他しこの比率は時
期的手れ及び對象の相異等のため多少不正確な點もあるが大體の組織率とみてよいであらう。
産業大分期別に列号すると次の通りである。
農業18%、林業379%、水產業16%、鐵業949%、建工業74%、製造工業51%、不電氣水
道業9636、業209%、金業7096、運通信業10823、-業146、自山業78%、公
務及量102%。

280.159人（45%）の順きある。規模別の組織率を正確なる数学を以て示すととは出来ないが。

れてみると行做すことが川来る。
8. 經微别報合結成狀况
組合組織の主後を、一事業又は同一余業系続の事業所のみの勞的で組織するか、事業所、地
くは職業に関係なく同一業の勞例者のみで組機するか、同一職業のみの勞働者で組織する
小、或は企業、產業、業に開係なく地域單位の勞的者で組織するか、卽も企業別組織、產業別
組織、業別組織、共の他の組織の4分に分けると、企業別組織が倒的で、全組合数の
880%、全組合日数の81.796をめてみる。業別組機は組合数の 41%、組合日数の 6.4%で
少であるが比教的製造二業の機技器工業に多い。職業別組織は組合数の 69％組合員数の
は6るで、中業別組織よりやへ多い。白山業の中の教育に題著であり、向職業の性置上建設工業
に多い。の他の組微は装だ僅少にして、組合数1.0%、組合日数2.2％である。

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.81)
6
勞働組合調查要綱
1. 調の 日的
我國勞働組合の組織状況及び話混を産業別、府縣別に詳細に調査しその質體を明らかにす
ることを目的とする。
2. 調の時期
昭和十二年十二月日现
3. 調查の範同
1. 單位勞働組合（調在票は單位券停組合調在票を使用方る）組合法第七條に基く獨自の組合規
約を有し獨自の話当をなし得る最下級單位の券的組合で共の局出をすでになしたものは勿論
本登録のものでも現に務組合が設立されている場合は必す調査する。
2. 券組合連合團證（調查票（は券組合連合團體調查票及構成組合名調查票を使用する）
、勞働組合連合開證
、勞像組合協議會（勞的組合（連合関體を含む）の組織する常
設的な速絡協置會
4. 查の方法
(1）組合集に調査票を作成よる但し所單一組織の組合については、本部地方連合會、地方評議會、
地方協議命及び若の支部くは分については出の有無にかいはらす便宜上次のような取扱
いたする。
1. 國级勞働組合
国券但组合、同地方程議會、同管理部及び同工機部支部（数工場の勞働
き組するもののみ）の調は券働組合連合国能調在票を用いる。
管理部支部に的する分合及び工機部支部（一事業場のみの勞例者で組織す
るもののみ）に場する組合の調在は單位勞偶組合調査票を用いる。
2. 全日本電策率管券低組合
会日本電序業券偶組合、同地方本部及同府縣支部の調查は券
個組合連合國警票を用い同分會は單位勞例組合調在票を用いる。
3. 全選信從業日組合
理信從業員組合、同地方連合會、同地區協義會の調售は勞的組合連
各票を用い、同交部調は單位券組查票を用いる。
4. 日有教装日组合
本部及府日合の調券組合連合開體调票を用い多、府
数日組つ下部は各部道府源によりしく組歳が異つてるので
各官部にを券偶組合連合國體と單位券例組合とに區別して調査する。
5 共の他の所謂單一組合（地方的は府事的な單一組合も含む）についても同出の有無に
かはら手二華在する。例之会日本機器及び同支部等は勞假組合
運合は體として报い分會は之を單位券個組合として扱う。
(2) 调作楼系统
勞働省勞働統計局查局—部道時縣——勞政事務所—開售警當者—組合代表者。

September 30, 1948
894.
MEMORANDUM FOR THE UNITED STATES MEMBER,
FAR EASTERN COMMISSION
5043/9-3048
Subject: Principles for Japanese Trade Unions (FEC O45/5)
At the meeting of the Commission on 23 September 1948 the
Chinese Ambassador inquired about the extent to which paragraph 4
of the above policy decision had been implemented in Japan prier
to the Supreme Commander's letter of July 22, 1948. The Representa-
tives of New Zealand and India associated themselves with this
inquiry.
In response to this question, you are authorized to provide
the Conmission with the information contained in the attached stat
ment.
Following the presentation of this information to the Commission,
if any member should inquire as to the effect of General MaaArthur's
letter of July 22, 1948 on the Labor Relations Adjustment Law, you
are authorized to call attention to the statment of the U. S. Member
of the Allied Couneil, as set forth verbatim in FEC 318/1 of 21
September 1948, to the effect that General MacArthur phrased his
letter "as a suggestion rather than a directive".
If inquiry is made as to the effect of the Japanese Cabinet
CSN
ordinance of July 31, 1948 on this legialation, you are authorised to
state that this interim meesure temporarily suspended the application
of the "Labor Relations Adjustment Law" to government employees.
If further pressed as to the effect of the panding revisions of
Japanese laws, which are t& be introduced in the Diet, on the "Labor
Relations Adjustment Law", you are authorized to state that this
question is under discussion between SCAP and the Japanese government
894.5043
and that you are unable to provide any information on the subject at
this time.
(2gn) P.P. Clailau, Jr.

=== Page 202 === (confidence 0.97)
STATEBIT EECARDDH
IMPIENRPTATION 0V PRO 045/5
September 20, 1946, was promulgated and put into foree on Csteber 13
The salient features of this Act are as follors:
The primary responsibility for settling disputes is stated to
lie with the parties involved, the Government's role being to assist
them. On occurrence of an act of dispute i.e. one hampering the
normal activities of an enterprise, the parties are imeediately to
report to the appropriate Labor Relations Committee. These Cemmittees,
organised on national, prefectural and in some cases on a speeial basis,
are tripartite bodies consisting of equal members or represemtatives
of employers, amployees and neutral (1.e. government meleeted) msmbers.
The Labor Relations Committees, assisted by the competent
government authority, are responsible for the conduet of the eom-
ciliation, mediation and arbitration activities called for under
the Act. Each Committee maintains a panel of conciliators, named on
the basis of experience in and knowledge of labor relations matters.
In the event of a dispute and upon the request of one or both parties
or on his own initiative, the chairman of the appropriate Labor Rela-
tions Committees appoints a conoiliator from this panel whe will
endeavor to contact both parties, ascertain their viems and assist
them in arriving at a settlement. If no settlement can be reached, the
conciliator withdraws and reports the salient facts to the Laber
Relations Committees.
When certain conditions are present, and partioularly in cases
of disputes in public welfere workers such as transport, post, tele-
graph, telephone, gas, electricity and public bealth, mediation is
attempted by a Mediation Committee appointed by the Labor Relatiens
Committee concerned from its members. Acceptancs of any mediation
settlement is voluntary, but public opinion is mobilised by the
Mediation Committee for its support.
Where both parties agree in advance, or in accordance with a
collective bargaining agreement, Labor Relations Committses are
empowered to arbitrate disputes. acceptance of the arbitratien
swards are compulsory.
Certain restrictions are placed on acts of disputes under the
Act in question. Police, firemen, prison guards and those govern-
mental employees engagod in administrative or judiciary work are
forbidden the right to strike. Employees of Govermment-owned enter-
prises, such as the Government railways, postel, telephone and
tolograph

=== Page 203 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 •
telegraph systems, tobacso factories and camphor and salt memopolies
are exempted from this restriction.
Both sides to disputes in publie welfare activities are forbidden
to commit acts of dispute within 30 days after application for media-
tion has been made.
Employers are forbidden to discharge or discriminate against
workers for participation in acts of dispute, or for testifying
before a Mediation or Arbitration Committee.
Experience since the promulgation of this Law has demonstrated
that the Central and Prefectural Labor Relations Committees have been
by far the most important agency for settlement of labor disputes
which were not resolved by voluntary negotiations. Detailed statistics
showing the mumber of labor disputes in whioh the Labor Relations
Committees assisted in settlement, during eaeh month from December B46
through April 1948, are shown in a table presented in the momthly
"Summation of Non-Military Activities in Japan" for June 1948 (page
219). This table also indicates for each month the mmber of eases
and mmber of workers involved in disputes accompanied by strikes
or other "actual disputes" and the mumber of cases settled without
strikes or actual disputes.
The same table also presents a summary breakdown of labor
disputes in which other third parties assisted in settlement, such
as the government labor officials, representatives of other trade
unions, and members of political parties. It is noteworthy that
cases resolved by the Labor Relations Comaittees covered more than
99% of the total mumber of workers involved in labor disputes in
which there was third party intervention. Other signifieant faets
shown by the table are the following:
A. The mumber of cases handled by the Labor Relations
Committees has tended to increase steadily--ranging betmeen
18 and 33 cases per month in the first 6 months following
Deeember 1946, but covering more than 100 cases per month
during the last 4 months shown in the table. (Jamuary -
April 1948.)
B. The mumber of workers involved in labor disputes handled
by the Labor Relations Committees has been significant,
ranging between 1,750,000 and 2,467,000 each month from
October 1947 through April 1948.
C. In general the Labor Relations Committees have been
extremely suocessful in edjusting major labor disputes
without work stoppages or work disruptions. There have
boon

=== Page 204 === (confidence 0.93)
in cases handled by the Laber Relations Committees. De wesk et
last monthe covered by the table, the Laber Belatdeme Semmdste
of all workers involved in labor disputes-with the single sss
tion of March 1 948, when a mumber of govermment workers engaged
in a 24-hour stoppage.
Further information on the activities of the Iaber malatiems
Committees, the volume of cases handled by the Comeittees and the
cases resolved without "actual disputes" will be foed in earlier
issues of the monthly "Summatiom of Non-Military Astivities in
Japan".

The acting Political dviser has the anor to enciose
one copy of the Japanese text of a notice rom the Japanese
Ministry of Lebor to prefectural governors?together wth
a translation, which revises the Welfare, Transportation
and Home Ministries Ordinance No. 1 of 1946 (as previously
revised January 18 and March 14, 1948). The ordinance pro-
hibited officials and staff members of wartime labor
organizations from becoming officials of labor organizations.
The revision which became effective September 13, 1948
is designed to allow these individuals, in exceptional
cases, to become officials of labor organizations if it is
clear that they will contribute to the growth of a
democratic labor movement. Three important criteria are
to be considered especially before an individual is
exempted from the provisions of Ordinance No. 1 of 146:
Lile
time labor organizations.
22
(2) His positive opposition to militarism
heeto
or ultra-nationalism.
(3) His possible contribution to the
20Y
democratic growth of labor organizations.
reea
according to the press, the original ordinance affected
CS/A
21,l95 officials and staff members of wartime labor organiza-
tions. Included among the prominent men who may no longer
be subject to the ordinance are Mr. NaGaE Kazuo, Minister of
agriculture and Forestry, Mr. MaSaKI Kiyose, Vice Minister
of Commerce and Industry, and Mr. YOSHITaKh Keiichi, former
JAN 12 1940°
Vice Minister of Labor.
It is our opinion that this revision is intelligent
recognition of the injustice of blanket prohibitions
stipulated by such ordinances as the one just revised.
Furthermore, we believe this belated realization that all
officials and staff members of wartime labor organizations
were not necessarily guilty of militaristic and ultra-
nationalistic acts and attitudes is more in keeping with

Article 101. (Paragraph 3) Personnel of the service
shall not, while receiving pay of the National Govern-

prescribed by rules of the Authority.

The Dept. concurs with the opinion of Mr. Hepler, Chief
of the Labor Division, ESs, that it would be undesirable
to authorize a Japanese labor delegation to attend a meet-
ing of the World Federation of Trade Unions. It concurs
likewise that it would be desirable that denial of a request
that a delegation be permitted to go be made on some
technical basis in order to lessen the effectiveness of
propaganda use of the denial by the Communists.
一
No general congress of the WFTU is presently scheduled,
the split within the organization being such that it has
2
been impossible to proceed with arrangements for holding a
2948
meeting in the place of the one originally set for Brussels
in December 1948.
The Executive Bureau, however, is meeting on January 17.
At this latter meeting the future of the organization will
in all probability be decided, and there are strong proba-
bilities that there will be no general congress of the
organization as at present constituted. The possibility of
a "rump" session of course remains. In light of this situ-
C
ation it would not appear necessary, at least for the
CS
present, to give the Japanese labor leaders a definitive
answer to any request to be permitted to attend WFTU meeting.

The Honorable
2
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to invite the Department's
attention to this Mission's note of November 16, 1948,
to the American Embassy in Brussels (copy to Department)
concerning plans of certain Japanese labor organizations
to send delegates to the Brussels Congress of the World
800.
Federation of Trade Unions (originally scheduled to take
place during the first half of December, 1948), and to
transmit herewith a translation of an article from
544.01
Akahata (the Japan Communist Party daily) of November 19,
1948 on this subject.
According to the Akahata article, plans are under
ACT:.N
way to elect a number of delegates for the World Federa-
tion of Trade Unions Congress from among various Japanese
COFIES
labor unions. The election of delegates is expected to
C
TO:
be completed by January 15, 1949. The article further
FE
notes that the preparatory group representing the labor
unions has conferred with Mr. Chester W. HEPLER, Chief
EuR.
of the Labor Division, Eoonomic and Scientifio Section
General Headquarters, in regard to the problem of sendinga
0
delegates to the Brussels Congress.
frh
An officer of this Mission has been informed by
Mr. Hepler that he recently received a communist delegation,
headed by Mr. WATANABE Michio, which claimed that it
represented Japanese labor interests. The group requested
Mr. Hepler's assistance in securing approval by the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers for the proposed
Japanese labor mission to Brussels.
As the group which called on the Chief of the Labor
Division had no information as to the date of the Brusbels
Congress (the date originally soheduled in December
wAs
having
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 226 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despateh No.- 751,
November 29, 1948.
2
CONFIDENTIAL
-2-
having been postponed because of organizational diffioulties),
and had not yet adopted a definite procedure for the
election of delegates for the Congress, Mr. Hepler informed
the group that he was unable to consider its proposal.
Mr. Hepler believes that it would be most undesirable
to authorize a Japanese labor delegation to attend the
World Federation of Trade Unions meeting. He believes,
however, that it will be advisable to rejeot the proposal
on the grounds that it is impossible to convert Japanese
yen currenoy for such a purpose or on any other technical
basis in order that the communists cannot make political
capital out of the issue. Mr. Hepler shares the belief
of this Mission that the communist labor leaders have been
convinced from the beginning that the Supreme Commander
will not au thorize such a mission, but have proceeded
with their plans for the sake of the publicity involved
and with the hope that if the Supreme Commander does
reject the proposal they can claim in their propaganda
that only reactionary elements are permitted to go abroad,
while representatives of the "democratic" labor movement
are denied such privileges.
Mr. Hepler also believes that there is a possibility
that the World Federation of Trade Unions Congress will
not take place due to the recent disintegration of that
organization, and that it may not therefore be neoessary
to give the Japanese labor leaders a definitive answer
on the matter.
In any event, there appears little likelihood that
a Japanese labor delegation will be authorized to attend
the World Federation of Trade Unions meeting. If such
a meeting does take place, there is still the possibility
that Japanese labor unions may be represented by Japanese
already abroad or by other nationals, as suggested in
this Mission's note of November 16, 1948 to the American
Embassy in Brussels.
Respectfully yours,
W. J. Sebald

Enclosure to Despatoh. No. 751
dated-November 29, 1948 from
the United States Politieal

the subject, "Proposed Japanese

(COPY)
Japan Delegates to WFTU to Be Elected by Laborers
Akahata, November 19, 1948. Translator: H. Akutsu.
Full translation:
Preparations by the Japanese labor unions to dispatch
their delegates to the World Federation of Trade Unions
are making good progress. The first meeting of the
executive committee for sending the delegates to the
record convention of the WFTU was held Wednesday after-
noon at 13O0 hours at the ZENKYOIKU KAIKAN (All Eduoation
Building), and measures by which to realize the plan
were discussed.
Attending the meeting were representatives of
approximately 40 unions, including the Japan Eleotrio
Power Workers Union, the All Japan Electric Industry
Workers Union, the All Japan Coal Workers Union, the
National Council of Coal Mine Workers Unions, the
National Council of Government Employees Unions and
the National Public Corporations Employees Union. The
representatives decided that all future moves should
be directed to the removal of all obstacles lying in
the way. It was also agreed to have the neutral unions
represented at the meeting again ask the Japan Federation
of Trade Unions to participate in the project.
The essential points approved at the meeting follows:
1. The delegates shall be elected direoly by the
workers of the country, Most of the candidates shall
be those recomended by individual unions and backed
by the executive committee.
2. Candidates are to be recommended by the unions
by 10 December and the election completed by 15 January
and the results are to be published on 20 January.
3. The election methods to be used in future
general elections shall be disoussed by a suboommittee
of the convention. The Federation of Tokyo Metropolitan
Office Workers Unions, the All Japan Electric Industry
Workers Union, the Ali Japan Communications Workers Union,
the Tokyo Metropolitan: Office Employees Union and the
Metal Industry Workers Union have already sel ected their
candidates for the delegation. The Japan Electric Power
Workers Union is also choosing its candidates.
Representatives

executive committee for dispatohing Japanese delegates
to the World Federation of Trade Unions oalled on
Chief Chester W. Hepler of the GHQ Labor Division to
disouss the delegation problem. Division Chief Hepler
told the representatives to keep him informed on the
progress of the oampaign. The representatives promised
to comply with the Division Chief's request.

SEORET
TO
NA - Mr Bishop
DATE: January 28, 1949
FROM : NA - Mr. Green
suBject: Japanese Government Workers Labor Legislation: Summary
VR
1. On July 22, 1948 General MacArthur stated 1n a letter
894.504
to Prime Minister Ashida that the draft public service legisla-
tion was deficient in certain respects. He went on to say in
effect that it was his view that Government workers should not
be permitted to strike and except for those public workers
00
emplcyed in Government corporations (which he defined as rai
ways, salt, camphor and tobacco) should not be granted the
right of collective bargaining "in its usual coercive sense"
Government employees should seek a redrese of grievances or
betterment of their working conditions through petition, coll
tive if so desired, to the newly created National Personnel
Authority, which would be the ultimate court of appeal. As
result of SCAP's letter, 2.7 million Government workers were
deprived of the "right" to strike and only 570,000 of them
retained collective bargaining rights. Messrs. Killen and
Stanchfield of SCaf's Labor Section resigned over the denial
of collective bargaining rights. One significant point abou
General MacArthur's letter is the implication that the curbi
of Government workera' right to strike is justifiable in a
6
country where the police are not backed up by national armed
forces.
2. On July 31, 1948 a Japanese Government ordinance
based on General MacArthur's letter was issued. This ordinarge
swept away all strike and collective bargaining rights for al
categories of Government workera, thus going beyond the purpet
of SCAP's letter. At the same time all Government labor conz
tracts under negctiation were cancelled. This ordinance was
to remain in effect until the new National Public Service Law
was enacted.
3. WAR 87563 to SCAP (13 August 1948). State-Labor
expressed concern over Cabinet order because it (a) denies
collective bargaining rights to workers outside normal civil
service categories, (b) cancels mutually negotiated labor con-
tracts and (c) denies right to strike in those Government enter-
prises where there is no public interest involved (tobacco,
S
camphor). Further information desired.
4.

e) Curtailment of right to strike may be justified in an

7. WAR 88625 to CINCFE (September 1, 1948). Forwards Seoretary
Royall's statement to the press:" SCAp's recommendations in his letter
to Ashida, "as we understand them, do not in any way confliot with
FEC policy." SCAF requested to forward legislation to Washington for
comment prior to final passage by Diet.
Proposal: (1) SCAP should be requested to revoke his "directive"
of July 22 and the ordinance of July 31 as being contrary to
FEC-014/9 and 045/5.
(2) Repressive measures against the Japanese Government
workers should be discontinued.
9. WAR 90494 to SCAP of October e, 1948.
State, Army, Labor
desire SCAp's consideration of following comments as expression of
US policy interest on this problem.
a) FEC-O45/5 should apply at least to industrial workers in
the Government employ. This interpretation would relieve US of
probably having to exercise its unilateral veto in FEC.
b) SCAP should make finding that Government strikes would
"directly prejudice the objectives and needs of the Occupation".
This would be consistent with paragraph 5 of O45/5. This would
not prejudice question for future.
c) All induetrial workers in Government employ should be
granted collective bargaining, not ineluding the coercive power
of the strike threat. This would give them recourse to mediation
and arbitration machinery.
d) Government employecs should have the right to form and
join lawful and non-subversive employee organizations of their own
choosing and to present grievances and negotiate through representa-
tives with proper authorities.

10. SCAP C 64458 of October 12, 1948. Refutation of above.
Everything SCAP has done is solidly based on US concepts and prac-

within scope of SCAP's executive authority. All, including

extreme left wing of the Socialists, fully support present law

by the rank and file. The entire charccter of the labor movement

meets views of Japanese themselves and is in conformity with NSC

greater degree of liberaliem on thie conquered and occupied country
during its almost primitive and tuitionary stage in the absorption
of democracy". Unless the labor movement is charted on a careful
and mcderate course it will soon destroy itself and doom to certain
failure all efforts toward economic rehabilitation and the building
of a truly rponsible government.
Regarding choosing representatives from among those in service,
against will of SCAP, Japanese themeelves insisted on inclusion of

In its very immaturity in Government under democratic process
and in its present impoverished economic state Japan is infinitely
more vulnerable tc the pressuree of social unrest than could be
expected in the Anglo-Saxon countries, France, etc. with their
traditions of crucrly and reeponsible government.

My dear Mr. Secretary:
In accordance with our conversations concerning
894.5043/3
my recent trip to Japan, I am attaching a copy of the
report on this trip which I have submitted to the Secretary
of the Army.
The report stresses the importance of a sound
and democratic Japanese trade union movement for the
development of democracy in Japan, and the need for
adopting propèr measures which would strengthen democratic
tendencies in the labor movement and, at the same time,
retard the communist upsurge within the trade unions.
It is my considered judgment that these rec-
ommendations will assist greatly in the achievement of
our goal of democratizing Japan.
Secretary Tobin has also transmitted this report
to the Secretary of State. After Mr. Acheson has had
CS/FL
an opportunity to consider it, I should be glad to discuss
my recommendations with him at his convenience.

Under sueh a program price increases or subsidies would be granted

48 HOUR WEEK FOR GOVERNHENT EHPLOYERS
This drastic and abrupt chenge in the face of umemployment was
totally unjustified. I strongly urge that immediately the workweek for
Government esployees be reduced to not more than a net 40-hour workweek
and that continued study be given to determine whether a further reduotion
in the workweek is advisable. (If this is done, using the reasoning
that unemployment is a justification, it would prove most helpful in r
gaining the confidence of the Japanose workers.)
COMMONISM IN JAPANESE UNIONS
Many top union positions in Jajan are occupied by Commmists,
or by people following the Communist Party line. This situation bas growm
to serious proportions and shouli receive immediate attention. I recommend
as follows:
1. That a committee of prominent trade union leaders from the
United States as proposed by Secrstary Tobin be sent to Japan immediately
with an adequate secretariat and staff. That they tour Japan, visit with
Japanese labor leaders, and speak to labor audiences, in an effort to
improve democratic practices in Japanese trade unions and expose the
techniques and practices of the Comaumists.
2. That resources, such as paper, mimeograph machines, printing
and other essentials be made available to anti-Communist forces working
inside the Labor movement in Japan.
3. Discontinuance of the practice of appointing Communists to
to Government positions, but have Labor given adequate recognition through
non-Communist, genuinely democratic labor leaders.

STANDARD FORM NO. 64
TO
: FE ● ir. Butterworth
DATE: April 12, 1949
FROM : NA - Mr. Bishop
tral
SUBJECT: Sending of Committee of U.S. Labor Leaders to Japan.
S
.ith reference to the recormendation, attributed to assistant Secretary
of Labor Gibson, that an American abor Group visit Japan, it is recommended
that the position of this office be as follows:
(1) The sending of any labor mission to Japan at this juncture should
be discouraged for the reasons advanced by General lacArthur and by Mr. Dodge.
(2) As a counter proposal, consideration should be given to the appoint-
ment by the Department of Labor of an able Labor Adviser to General MacArthur,
who would have the confidence of SCAr, the Department of State and the Depart-
to
ment of the Army.
8
6
4
It is recognized that something tangible must be done to meet the growing
criticism of the other FEC countries, as well as of influential segments of 
U.S. opinion, toward the labor policies presently pursued by SCAP in Japan. O
There is already an Australian policy proposal before the FEC Steering Committee,
supported by almost all the other countries, which, if adopted, will counter-W
mand major sectors of the National Public Service legislation which was passed
by the Diet at SCAP's request.
4
We have long adhered to the view that one way to meet this criticism
would be the reorganization of the Japanese Government's telecommunications N
service as a public corporation with its employees' being accorded the arbitr
tion and mediation facilities given other public corporation workers. IndeedO
it is believed that such a move is desirable from a substantive point of view.
However, SCAP has persistently refused to adopt this suggestion which was
first made to him last October; and it is not likely that he would now accept
it in view of his interpretation of the present political and economic situa-
tion in Japan. This places us tactically in a difficult position because we S
cannot direct SCAr on the details of his tasks, we cannot dispute with him
the potential dangers of any particular suggestion, and we cannot place our- O
selves in the position of telling him to do something against his better
judgment in the matter. Our best hope therefore lies through the appointment
of a Labor Adviser who will be qualified through professional experience in
labor matters as well as through direct, detailed knowledge of the situation
in Japan to give the Supreme Commander and this government sound, practical
advice.
Other measures--such as the elimination of the 48-hour week for government
workers, the modification of the penal provisions in the National Public Service
Law, etc.

=== Page 260 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Law, etc.--have also been su gested as means of assuaging criticism of
SCAF labor policies, but it is felt thet none of these would be of suf-
ficient scope to be adequate.
The appointment of a competent Labor Adviser to SCAr could do much to
iprove democratic practices in Japanese trade unions and to expose the
techniques and operations of the Communists. At the same time his appoint-
ment would reassure the Japanese of our concern for the development of a
strong, democratic labor movement. It would be a factor in counteracting
the mounting criticism in the FEC and elsewnere of our labor policies. If
such an appointment were made in time, we might have some hope of countering
the Australian policy proposal without being placed in the unfortunate position
of having to do so tnrough exercise of the veto.
It is therefore suggested that if and when you are called upon to dis-
cuss this matter with representatives of the Department of the Army, you
indicate that we are willing to support General ...acArthur and the epartment
of the Army in opposing the sending of a Labor Mission to Japan at this time.
It is also sugested tnat you propose a joint State-Army reply cable be
sent to SChr alon: the rollowing lines:
(1) numerate the difficulties we see facing our labor policies
in Japan, i.e., the critical attitudes of labor groups in the U.S.,
the growin: international criticism including the current Australian
proposal in the rC, the resentment of the Japanese labor movement;
(2) State that we believe these criticisms could be resolved,
without upsetting the situation in japan, through allowing the
Japanese to amend the MrSL within the confines of certain broad
principles in order to recognize some of the objections to the Law
offered by ir. Gibson (4O-hour week for overnment workers,
estaolishment of a telecommunications corporation, etc.);
(3) State that however much we may consider these NrsL revisions
to be desirable and feasible we would not wish CAr to go against
his better judgment for we are in no position in ashington to
appraise all the aspects of the problem;
(4) State that we believe that, wnile the appointment of a Labor
Mission to Japan ould be helpful in ccmbatting Communism in
Japanese trade unions, in furthering democratic labor practices, and
in bringing an experienced labor viewpoint to the NPSL controversy,
etc., State and Army reco nize the validity of SCAF's reasons for
opposing such a  ission;
(5) State that we believe all the advantages and none of the dangers
of a labor mission could be achieved by appointment of a Labor Adviser
(filling in with arguments such as those raised above);
(6) State

=== Page 261 === (confidence 0.94)
-3-
(6) State that the early appointment of a Labor Adviser would be
a factor in countering the critical attitudes of the FEC countries
and labor groups here and might enable us to prevent the Australian
proposal being defeated only by the lone veto of the U.S. delegate
in the FEC.

RE: Decentralization of Tokyo Shibaura Electric Co., Ltd.
Recently, the Tokyo Shibaura Electric Co., Ltd., has re-
ceived from the Holding Liquidation Comnission, a draft of the
final directive, ordering its decentralization under Iw 207-1947-
Decentralization of Economic Power. Following is the ist of the
crder:-
1
In order to facilitate the decentralization of excessive
economic power embodied in the Tokyo Shibaura Elec-
tric Co., 28 of its factories and laboratories must
be detached.
2.
However, a merger is permitted of its sponsored com-
pany, the Toshiba Rolling Stock Co., Ltd. (Toshiba
Sharyo K.K.).
We understand that the policy of decentralization has been
altered somewhat in cases where such action is detrimental to pro-
duction and the welfare of Japanese economy.
Followin: are some reasons which compel us to oppose the de-
tachment of the 28 factories after thorough scientific research
by experts.
The entire 44 factories of the Shibaura Electric
Co., are strongly interwoven in a reciprocal re-
lationship and the severing of the factories can
only end in disorganization and added expenditure
with nothing to gain in the end. The remaining fac-
tories cannot be speedily reorganized and the re-
sultant loss of time alone is contrary to the reali-
zation of the Nine Principles laid down by SCAP for
Japan's recovery.
2. Enterprises of a vital character which will suffer:
1。
Supplies for Occupation Forces.
2。
11
II
Domestic use-vital.
3.
11
11
Electric Power Industry.
4
11
11
Railways.
5。
11
11
Steel mills, etc.
3
The merger of the Poshiba Rolling Stock Co., is a
direct contradiction of the Decentralization Law
for it rather encourages centralization of monopo-
listic economic power. All workers are well aware
of the
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 268 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to
Tokyo's Despatch No. 216,
April 12, 1949.
-3-
above fact and kelieve that the measures taken are
for the bene fit of management. Therefore, we bring
these facts to your attention, as we know that your
interest in the development of Japan to be sincere.
4-
If there is need for dismembering the company it may
be livided into two parts:- Heavy electrical industry
and light electrical industry. This suggestion will
elininate upheaval and save untold misery, time and
money. There is comparatively weak reciprocity in
the relation between heavy and light electrical manu-
facturing at the Shibaura Electrical Company.
5.
Lastly, but not the least of all, it must be borne in
mind that 7,000 workers will be thrown on the streets.
iven with their present income life has reached the
stage wherein they barely are able to keep alive,
therefore, the discharge of the workers will be
fought fiercely. Those divorced from the lives of
the people, although well-meaning, are often found to
pass upon legislation that throttle their very lives.
The entire background of the oppression of the Japa-
nese workers before the war and their present situa-
tion must be taken into consideration.
6,
Japanese workers cannot go backward; they cannot re-
main static; there is only one path then to follow--
they must go forward, just as the rest of Asia is doing--
striving for a better life! Without a doubt you can
easily see that the elimination of the 28 factories
merely means that various selfish interosts wish to
lower the standards of the Japanese workers--that means
to kill them. This we know is not your wish.

Chairman of the Central Executive Committee
Federation of the Toshiba Unions,
sole organization
greatest number of Toshiba employees.

REO:
The Eoard tecomaends that this Company not be
Reguired to Divide into Separate Companies,
but that it be Ordered to Dispose of 27 Plants,
and that it he Permitted to Hetain its Interest
in Two Subsidiaries
J. Pvo Shibaura Denki K. K. is a large nanufacturer of elec-
trir mac! inery, eguipment ani appliances. It is one of the three
large companies i Japan producing heavy electric power apparatus and
a conplate line of electrical equipment and appliances. As a long
line producer, this company's products closely parallel those of its
two principal conpetitors, Mitsubishi Denki and the Hitachi Seisakusho
i. h.*, except that Pokyo Shibaura is a leading producer of electrie
lamps and vacuun tuhes. The company owns 43 plants of which 35 were
acqaired during or subsequent to 1940. It employs approximately.30,000
people and las about 14, 000 stockholders. The HCLC holds shares or
voting rights representing approximately 38,; of the company's out-
standing stock.
2, The Board has reviewed the basic file, which included a
draft of Proposed Order of Reorganization (November 1948) recommended
by SS/AC and separate menorande submitted to the Board containing
the comments and recommendations of HCLC. The Board has considered
also a memorandum from ESS/AC dated 8' November 1948 relating to re-
straints of trade in the electrical equipment industry of Japan and a
memorandum from ESS/AC dated 15 January 1949 commenting upon recommen-
dations of HCLC. In addition the Board has conferred with the Industry
Division of ESS, Civil Comunications Section, officials of the HCLC
and the company, inspected four of company's main plants, and has stu-
died the competitive situation in the principal lines of the company's
business.
3. The draft of the Proposed Order of Reorganization recommended
by ss/Ao finds that Pokyo Shibaura Denki K. K. is an excessive con-
centration of economic power ithin the meaning of Law 207, and orders
the
* See Board's recomnendatio. to SCAP in the Hatter of Mitsubishi Denki
31 December 1948 and Hitachi Seisakusho Kabushiki Kaisha 24 January 1949.

-2-
the elimination of such excessive concentraticn of power by the
estahlishment of five successor companies and the dissolution of
the existing company.
4. Included in the factors mentioned by ESS/AC bearing on
tie desi nation of the company were: the acquisition of many plants
subsequent to l939, its large size and productive capacity, owner-
ship of stock in many other companies, its former Zaibatsu connect-
ions, together xith restraints of trade and monopolistic practices,
ani membership in control associations.
5 fhe oiC does not agree with the plan for breaking up the
com any as pruposed by BSS/Ac but recomrends that Shibaura be or-
dered to dispose of its plants.
6. In its previous reccrmendations the Eoard discussed the rel-
ative competitive positions of Hitachi, Mitsubishi Denki and Shibaura
as the three long line producers of electrical equipment and appli-
ances. The statistics furnished the Board indicate that Shibaura
faces strong competitien from the other two long line companies and
from other aore specialized producers in all important items produced
by it Shibaura in the past under certain conditions appears to have
manufactured as high as eighty (80) percent of the vacuum tubes pro-
iuced in Japan. Since the conclusion of the war, however, a large num-
ber of concnies have entered this field. Although at present Shi-
baura produces approximately 35.s of the Japanese output, its relative
position has been greatly reduced and the companies now engaged in the
manufacture of these items should insure a reasonable degree of com-
petition in the future.
7 The Board concurs in that portion of the Proposed Order as
recomended by SS/AC which finds Shibaura to be an excessive con-
centration of economic power within the meaning of Law 207. The
Board does not agree with the recommendation of ESs/AC that the com-
pany be reorganized into five successor companies. In general the
proposed reorganization would require the segregation of the company's
accivities by transferring to separate companies the lamp and tube
business, radio equipment and parts, electrical machinery and equip-
ment, clectric fixtures and appliances, and dies and cutters. The
Board does not believe that this action is required by law or that it
would inprove the competitive situation in these industries to any
appreciable degree.
8. The Loard recognizes, however, that if Shibaura were to
utilize fully its existing plant facilities it would be in a posi-
tion to exercise a far greater influence in its respective fields
than is indicated by its present production, The Board concurs with
the recommendation of HCiC that the company be required to dispose
of certain plants, many of which are now idle or are being operated
on a reduced scale. This action should not only make such plants and
equipment available for use by competing enterprises or for other
purposes
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 271 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclcsure No. 2 to
Tokyo's Despatch No. 216,
April 12, 1949.
- 3 -
purposes, but should also, together with the action ccntemplated
with respect, to the disposition of the company's affiliates under
other laws and ordinances, limit substantially the extent to which
Shibaura might restrict or control competition ii the future.
9. Two of Shibaura's subsidiaries, Toshiba Sharyo and Kyodo
Tatemomo, are closely related to the company's operations. The'se-
curities of these companies, owned by Shibaura, are now held for dis-
position by the HCLC. Toshiba Sharyo, a wholly owned subsidiary of
Shibaura, owns and operates a plant which is located in the com-
pound of Shibaura's Fuchu Works, in which it builds electric loco-
moLives. Much of the engineering for the Sharyo company is performed
by Shibaura's engineering staff. Some of the buildings owned by
these two companies are devoted to common usage. This subsidiary
was organized in 1945 and acquired Shibaura's locomotive division
pursuant to an order issued by the former Japanese Government. Shi-
baura also has 60% of the stock of Kyodo Tatemono, which was organized.
to acquire and manage the office building in central Tokyo, previously
occupied by Shibaura. This building was built on land owned by Shi-
baura and is now occupied by the United States Army.
10. The Board recommends that this company not be required to
divide into separate companies, but that an Order be issued requiring
the company to dispose of the following plants, with the exception
of such tools or equipment as may be necessary to meet current Occu-
pation objectives: Asahikawa, Nagai, Kamo, Odasakae, Tsukagoshi,
Moji, Imabari, Tondabayashi, Same, Akita, Matsukawa, Niigata, (Niizu
Nuttari), Maebashi, Gumma, Mcbara, Adachi, Ebara, Shonan, Ohito,
Kawagishi, Kobe, Aboshi, Gosen, Washizu, Oigawa, Kariya and its bio-
physical labcratory. The Order should provide that the disposition
of these plants shall be supervised by HCLC.
11. The Board recommends that HCLC not dispose of the securities
of Toshiba Sharyo and Kyodo Tatemono, and that appropriate action be
taken to permit Tokyo Shibaura to retain its interest in these com-
panies or the property owned by them.

I, The Holding Company Liquidation Commission, hereinafter
referred to as the "Commission," having designated Tokyo Shibaura
Denki Kabushiki Kaisha hereinafter referred to as "Shibaura," as
an excessive concontration of economic power on 8 February 1948,
pursuan! to the powers granted to it by virtue of the provisions
of Arti L: 3 and in accordance with the standards adopted under
the pr Isions of Article 6 of Law Nc. 207 of 1947: Elimination
of Excessive Concentrations of Economic Power Law; Shibaura hav-
ing entered its aprearance to the proceedings by the filing of
formal notice pursuant to Article 14 of the Rules of Procedure is-
sued under saic Law; and having filed its statements with respect
to the applicability of the Standards for Excessive Concentrations
in Industrial Fields adopted pursuant to said Law.
2. HOW, THEREFORE, having considered such statements, docu-
ments, and recomendations as were filed by parties to the pro-
ceedings, and all of the facts before it, the Comrission finds
and orders:
3. Phat Shibaura is an excessive concentration of economic
pewer within the meaning of Law No. 207 of 1947: Elimination of
Excessive Concentrations of Ecoromic Power Law.
4. That Shibaura shall dispose of the following plants,
with the excertion of such tocls or equipment as may be neces-
sary to meet current Occupation objectives: Asahikawa, Nagai,
Kamo, Odasakae, Tsukagashi, Moji, Imabari, Tondabayashi, Same,
Akita, Matsukawa, Niitsu, Nuttari, Naebashi, Gumma, Motara, Ada-
chi, Ehara, Shonan, Chito, Kawagishi, Kobe, Aboshi, Gosen, Washizu,
Oigawa, hariya, and Dic-physical and Chemical Laboratory;. pro-
vided that the disposition of the above plants shall be supervised.
by and shall be subject to the approval of the Commission.
5. That Shibaura may reacquire the shares of stock in Toshiba
sharyo Kabushiki Kai sha now held by the Commission for the purpose

Re; Decentralisation of Tokyo Shibaura Electric Co., Ltd.
1.
Recently, the Tokyo Shibaura Electric Co., Ltd., has received
from the Holding Liquidation Commission, a draft of the final direct-
ive, ordering its decentralization under Law 207-1947-Decentralization
of Economic Power. Following is the gist of the ordert-
1. In order to faciliate the decentralization of ercessive
economic pouer embodied in the Tokyo Shibaura Electric
Co., 28 of its factories and laboratories must be
detached.
2.
lHowever, a merger is permitted of its sponsored company
the Toshiba Rolling Stock Co., Ltd. (Toshiba Shabyo K.K.).
We understand that the policy of decentralization has been
altered sonewhat in cases where such action is detrimental to pro-
duction and the welfare of Japanese economy.
Folling are some reasons which compel us to oppose the detach-
ment of the 28 factories after thorough scientific research by experts.
1.
The entire L4 factories of the Shibaura Electric Co.,
are strongly interwoven in a reciprdcal relationship and
the severing of the factories can only end in disorganization
and added expenditure with nothing to gain in the end.
The remaining factories cannot be speedily reorganized and
the resultant loss of time alone is ∞ntrary to the realiz-
ation of the Nine Principles laid down by SCAP for Japan's
recovery.
2.
Enterprises of a vital charachter which will suffer:
1.
Supplies for Occupation Forces.

3.
The merger of the Toshiba Rolling Stock Co., is a
direct contradiction of the Decentralization Law for
it rather encourages centralization of monopolistic
economic power. All workers are well aware of the above
fact and believe that the measures taken are for the
benerit of managenent. Therefore, we bring these facts to
your attention, as we know that your interest in the develop-
ment of Japan to be sincere.
