Note: Mr. Amis and a group of five Japanese visited Washington briefly after
attending the Pittsburgh meeting of the ILo Coal Mines Committee. The five 
Coal Board of the Commerce and Industry Ministry; H. Toramoto, chief of the
chief of the Lebor Department of the Mitsui Mining Company; Yasuhiro Goh, of
the Commerce and Industry Ministry, who served as interpreter; and Takeo Mutd,
chairman of the Japan League, of Coal Miners' Unions, the largest coal miners'
union with a membership at of 1948 of more than 260,000. Some of their obser-
vations in other conversations are reported parenthetically below.
Status of Labor Division
Mr. Amis stated that the Division is very weak in personnel having actual
trade-union experience. He hoped that, as a result of contacts between Messrs.
Muto and John L. Lewis, the UMW will supply a man for his staff to work with
the Japanese coal unions. He also expressed the hope that other Americen trade
union personnel may be added to his staff. In this connection, he held that
力
labor experts assigned to Japan should be integrated into the Labor Division
instead of having separate status, as has been suggested in the case of a pro-
posed AFL Far Eastern representative. He anticipated that labor representatives.)
with status independent of the Labor Division would multiply the latter's pro-
-blems by providing an alternative and possibly conflicting source of gaijance
4
7
COTIAL
0
6
6

=== Page 280 === (confidence 0.99)
CONBPTIAL
2
for Japanese labor. Mr. Sullivan questioned whether, especially in view of
the Killen episode, American trade-union personnel might be easily procurable
for staff positions in the Labor Division.
As for the proposal to send a trade-union delegation to Japan for a
short-term mission, Mr. Amis perceived no objections, apparently differing
with the SCAP position that acceptance of such a mission would make it dif-
ficult to refuse similar delegations from other FEC countries. Mr. Amis
suggested in this connection that even a Soviet trade-union delegation could
do little more then is already being done by the well-staffed Soviet mission
in Japan, which, he stated, maintains close liaison with Japanese labor leaders.
The status of the Labor Division within SCAP was indicated by Mr. Amis'
statement that the Division was by-passed during the formulation in Head-
quarters of the new Japanese budget. Since the new budget is an integral
part of the economic stabilization program and because of the impact of
both on the Japanese labor situation, Mr. Amis indicated that the Division
might well have been conaulted.
Economic Stabilization Program
Mr. Amis foresaw a period of turbulent labor relations for the next
several months as a result of the inauguration of the economic stabilization
program. (This view was separately supported by the Japanese in his party,
some of whom anticipated "chaos" favorable to Commmmist advances not only
in the labor movement but also generally. None of the group seemed to have
a clear idea of the ultimate benefits expected to accrue to Japan, having
their eyes fixed only on the near future.) Mr. Amis continued that he
personally welcomed the prospect as an opportunity for Japanese lahbr and
management to reach a definitive delimitation of the balance of bargaining
power, provided that SCAP did not step in to stop or prevent strikes. In
Mr. Amis' view, the trade-unions would benefit from losing a few strikes and
consequently gaining a realization of the limitations of their power. He
did not appear to be greatly concerned over the prospect of Commmist advances
in the trade unions on the basis of labor's unsatisfied grievances, apparently
feeling that the approach to the question of Communist influence in the unions
lay in a fundamental revision of trade-union organizational practices from
the ground up.
(The present coal strike in Japan illustrates some facets of the im-
pact of the stabilization program. According to the Mitsui representative,
the corporation finds it necessary to cut wages 20 percent in order to
survive without the prospect of subsidies, loans, or price adjustments to
help meet payrolls. The union chairman, asserting that average mine wages
are now slightly below the national average, declared that the workers could
not absorb the wage reduction at the same time that the cost of living is
due to rise, food prices by perhaps 30 percent. Both men realized that
loss of the strike by the union would tend to increase the danger of pos-
sible capture of the union by the Commmists through growth of Commnist
influence among the rank and file)

=== Page 281 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFEIAL
3
The Anti-Communist Movement in Trade Unions
The anti-Commmnist Democratization Leagues are regarded with some
reservations by Mr. Amis, who stated that they tend to be a purely political
movement and to lack a positive trade-union program. Their leadership and
organization is no more democratic than in most other Japanese trade unions,
which in the main are controlled from the top down by a handful of mart
and aggressive leaders. Some of the Democratization League leaders are de-
finitely reactionaries. In addition, Mr. Amis expressed misgivings over the
tendency of the Leagues to split existing unions, saying that while such
splits may be inevitable and have some desirable effects they tend not only
to weaken labor's position as a whole but also to increase the Labor Division's
difficulties in developing strong and sound unionism.
Asked about the developing tie between the anti-Commumist labor movement
and the Socialist Party, Mr. Amis commented that Socialist prestige has fallen
so low that he doubted whether the party would make substantial progress in
gaining general labor support for some time to come. He considored it in-
evitable, however, that Japanese trade umions will resort increasingly to
political action, and stated that at the time he left Japan the Labor Divi-
sion was preparing a pamphlet for trade union dissemination recommending,
in effect, the type of political activity pursued by American trade unions.
(Comments of the Japanese labor leader, Mr. Muto, whose union is an
independent and has followed moderate policies, paralleled Mr. Amis' views
on the Democratization Leagues and the Socialist Party.)
Revision of the Trade Union and Labor Relations Adjustment Laws
Mr. Amis said that the Yoshida govermment produced draft revisions
that would have very seriously restricted existing labor rights and the
exercise thereof. He personally reviewed the drafts and struck out restric-
tive features. The bills presently before the Diet, according to Mr. Amis,
contain needed amendments designed to rectify a number of evils that have
arisen under the existing legislation. One of the most importent of these
is the great dependence of Japanese unions on employer support, specifically
in the form of salaries of en excessive number of full-time union officials,
all manner of facilities, and the use of company time for unnecessary union
business. Another undesirable practice is the inclusion in union membership
of personnel whose functions properly identify them with management. As a
result of such distortions, there has been a high degree of collusion be-
tween menagement and labor, as in an exemple mentioned by Mr. Amis of both
parties combining to force subsidies from the government.
The proposed revision of the labor laws would deny their protection
and benefits to unions not meeting standards which are clearly drawn with
respect to the above pointa. Enforcement would be through the labor relations
committees, which Mr. Amis considered adequate to the task. Mr. Amis stated
that he personally had screened all candidates for the Central Labor Relations
Committee and that local MG teams had followed suit for the prefectural com-
mittees. The inclusion of such few Commnists as are to be found among the
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 282 === (confidence 0.98)
CONFIDENTIAL
4
labor mombers on the committees was felt to be necessary if the labor side
were to be representative. Only one of the seven labor members of the
present Central Labor Relations Committee is a Commmmist, compared to at
least two on the previous committee.
These and other projected revisions, in Mr. Amis' view, will not
adversely modify labor's status nor will they greatly hinder Communist
influence in labor. He found labor leaders generally pleased and relieved
with the final version, although they and the left wing parties will con-
tinue to oppose the chenges pro forma. Mr. Amis considers that the unions
generally are capable of raising the funds necessary to support their
officers and activities, and that they will benefit in the long run from
the resulting independence.
As for public service legislation, Mr. Amis commented that in his
opinion the right to strike should be restored to government enterprise
workers, for without that right collective bargaining is empty. He consi-
dered that restoration of the right to strike would hold no danger, for
as long as the occupation lasts it has the power to prevent or halt strikes
at any time, and once the occupation is over the Japanese will pass laws
to suit themselves. While the present public service legislation is in
effect, he stated, the Labor Division tends to oppose extending the scope
of the public corporation device to give govermment employees some labor
rights. The one exception was in the case of the Commmications workers,
who, he felt, should be given a status parallel to that of the govermment
railroad and monopoly employees.

SUBJECT: Australian Resolution Before FEC
The Australian Resolution, extending the provisions of
FEC O45/5 (Principles for Japanese Trade Unions) to workers
in Japanese Government enterprises, was passed by the
Steering Committee this morning by a vote of 6 to 1, the
United States opposing and the Netherlands, Philippines,
USSR and China abstaining. It is now before the Commission,
and an early vote is to be expected.
If adopted, the Australian Resolution would reverse
the position publicly taken by SCAP and seriously undermine 0
his prestige. I believe that, notwithstanding our objeetion (6
to certain aspects of the National Public Service Legisla- 4
tion, this Government is left with no course but to oppose 
the Australian Resolution if and when it comes to a vote.
It is accordingly recommended that the accompanying instruotion
be sent to General McCoy.

(which I understand Mr. Thorp is still holding), proposing

=== Page 304 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 392,
June 17, 1949.
- 2~
Japan, sent, it is understood, to various interested agencies,
including the Far Eastern Commission. The petition reiterates
the arguments put forth in an earlier petition from the Federation
of Toshiba Unions (forwarded with our despatch no. 2l6) and notes
that the case has aroused considerable controversy in the Diet,
the press, and the labor movement
The opposition of labor to the reorganization of Toshiba is
ostensibly based on a belief that the measure is in fact aimed at
streamlining rather than deconcentrating Toshiba. The Toshiba
Federation is clearly afraid of the mass unemployment likely to
result if 28 Toshiba plants are liquidated as contemplated in the
order, and believes that its bargaining position would be consider-
ably stronger if the problem were faced squarely as one of industrial
rationalization, rather than disglised as deconcentration and con-
sequently backed by the prestige and authority of the Deconcentration
Review Board.
Although the need for rationalization of the Toshiba Electric
Company is rather generally recognized, the question of whether
rationalization under the auspices of the Holding Company Liquidation
Commission (and by implication the Deconcentration Review Board)
is appropriate and ethical has come in for a good deal of discussion
in the press, and in the Diet. On April 28 and May 19, the Economic
Stabilization Committee of the House of Representatives held rather
inconclusive hearings on the subject, and on May 18 the Vice Premier
and the Attorney General were queried on the floor of the House of
Councillors regarding the motives behind the proposed order.
Mr. SASAYAMa Tadac. Chairman of the Holding Company Liquidation
Commission, who appeared as a witness before the House Committee,
was hard put to justify the order as a deconcentration measure. On
April 28, Representative MDA Masao, Demccratic Liberal, requested
Mr. Sasayama s opinions "precisely as to the point where the disposal
plan differs from the Company's rationalization plan." Considerable
criticism had been aroused, he said, by "the fact that in many
respects the decentralization plan calling for the disposal of 28
plants is identical with the Company's rationalization plan".
Mr. Sasayama stated only that the two plans were "not identical",
but offered no details supporting his argument. Subsequently, when
asked much the same question by Representative KATSUMATA Seiichi,
Social Democrat, r Sasayame again asserted that the proposed order
was not "entirely identical" with the Company's rationalization plans.
"In the case of enterprise adjustment, " he continued, "facilities of
disposed plants may be transferred to the main plants. In the case
of decentralization, however, this is out of the question.....It
cannot be gainsaid that decentralizetion has something in common with
the idea underlying enterprise adjustment, but I reject any suggestion
that the two are identical."
Mr. Sasayama's
RESTRICTED

=== Page 305 === (confidence 0.96)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 392,
June 17, 1949.
-3-
Mr. Sasayama's argument is somewhat weakened, however, by the
fact that transfers of machinery. rejected in his testimony as "out
of the question," are specifically provided for in the reorganiza-
tion order (transmitted with cur despatch no. 216), and are in fact
being carried out, according to representatives of the Toshiba
Federation of Labor Unions. Mr. Sasayama admitted, moreover, that
the "deconcentration" order was written with an eye toward possible
investment by the International General Electric Corporation in a
streamlined Toshiba. It would therefore appear that, if the
Federation's position is accepted as resistance against rationaliza-
tion disguised as deconcentration, rather than opposition to
rationalization or to deconcentration as such, its case is a strong
one.
Diet members also voiced misgivings regarding the reorganization
order on the grounds that it violates the four principles for de-
concentration laid down by the Deconcentration Review Board, especially
the principle that activity in numerous unrelated enterprises coes nct
in itself constitute evidence of excessive concentration of econcmic
power, as well as on the grcunds that amaigamation with Toshiba
Sharyo (Tokyo-Sh(baura Rolling Stcck Ccmpany) constitutes a step
towards increased industrial concentration in violation of Law no. 207.
The attempts of Mr. Sasayama and of Vice Premier HAYASHI Joji to
justify the proposed order were onsidered unconvincing.
Respectfully yours,
1
Cloyce K. Huston
Charge? d'Affaires ad interim

PETITION
May 25, 1949
Subject:
Deconcentration of Tokyo-Shibaura Electric Company,
Ltd., Kawasaki, Japan
To:
o
From
Makoto Kan, President, Congress of Industrial Unions
of Japan
Dear Sir:
On Feb. 18 1949, the Holding Company Liquidation Review
Commission has anncunced, in accordance with a draft directive
issued by the 5-member Review Board of Deconcentration commissioned
here by the esteemed Government of U.S.A.. disposition of 28 plants
of the Tokyo-Shibaura Electri: Company. Ltd., and of merger of the
Toshiba Rolling Stock Company to the former.
The draft directive. however, does not in any way meet the
aim of dimemberment of the Zaibatsu cliques, but, on the contrary,
it stimulates monopolistic concentration of enterprises in this
country. Moreover, the proposed disposition will result to
collapse production of the company giving rise to a lot of unem-
ployed.
As a result. the To-Shiba issue has raised many contro-
versies among Government members, in the Diet and on journalism.
It is now becoming a serious sccial and economic question in this
country.
The Congress of Industrial Unions of Japan is also strongly
interested in this problem of wrong implementation of the policy
under the Deconcentration Law.
Nevertheless, all prospects show an announcement of the
unaltered decisive order is to be made to the company in the
immediate nearness, Under the circumstances, we wish to draw
your attention on the matter and especially on what the Holding
Company Liquidation Commission had done in this country in
accordance with a directive of the aforementioned Board.
Eagerly expecting your expedite action, we remain,
Yours truly,
President, Congress of Industrial
Unions of Japan.

cited above, under the "Closed Institutions Ordinance"

In the first important decision by the Japanese Supreme
Court on a labor issue, the Supreme Court upheld the decision
of the lower courts in judging a certain employer guilty of
discriminatory treatment against his 90 employees disoharged
for engaging in dispute tactios. The Supreme Court ruled that
the actions of the employer brought pressure to bear on union
members and threatened to weaken the unity of the unions.
USPOLAD concludes that the Supreme Court has handed down a
liberal decision, but it remains for subsequent interpretations
and later issues to determine the extent of its application.

sUBJECT: Australian Proposal in FEC on Labor Policy in Japan.
AP
Dr. Blakeslee of the FEC advises that conversations with
Mr. Bullock of the Australian Embassy reveal that Mr. Bullock
intends to press for a vote on the Australian proposal for laber
policy in Japan at the FEC meeting on July 28, the last meeting
before the FEC summer recess. General McCoy has been trying Tot
a week to see the Australian Ambassador but has been unable ts 
make an appointment. Dr. Blakeslee gathers the impression fron
Mr. Bullock that Mr. Bullock is trying to prevent such a meeting
8
and to retain control in his own hands of the Australian labor
6
proposal.
On numerous occasions in the FEC the U.S. has made cloar
its opposition to the Australian proposal. The U.S. position
has never been presented directly to the Australian Government
through our Embassy in Canborra.
On June 1 of this year instructions were sent to the Uis.
representative on the FEC authorizing him to vote against the
Australian proposal if and when it is brought to a vote.
2
The vote in the Steering Committee to approve and forward
4
6
the Australian proposal was six in favor (Australla, Canada,
France, India, N.Z., and U.K.) to one opposed (U.S.) with four
abstentions (China, Netherlands, Philippines and the U.S.S.R.).
In view of the number of times and the lengthlat which we
have made clear our opposition to the Australian proposal at FEC
meeotings and in conversations with Australian, British and Canadian
representatives on the FEC, plus the fact that labor governments
in Australia and other commonwealth countries have special reasons
for supporting the Australians in their proposal, there is some
ground for argument that the best tactics for the U.S. would be
to treat the matter now in a matter-of-fact way and simply vote
against the proposal if it is brought to a vote. On the othor
hand, our opposition has been reported to the Australian Govern-
ment only through its representatives on the FEC. It therefore
seems useful to insure that the Australian Government has an
adequate direct exposition of our position.

SUBJECT:
Invitation to Non-Communist Labor Unions of Japan to
Join New World Labor Organization.
A wire from London (No. 3378, Aug. 25) reports that
the Preparatory Committee for the new international trade
union federation has invited a "Japanese trade union
center" (presumably the Japan Federation of Trade Unions,
or SODOiEI) and certain individual Japanese unions to an
organizational meeting scheduled for November. As you
8
6
are aware, this Department has looked with favor upon
94.5043/
the movement to organize this new body and has discreetly
encouraged broad United States labor participation.
SCAP has not been officially approached on this
matter and his current views regarding participation by
Japanese labor groups in international trade union meet-
ings are not known. In arch of this year, however, he
8
turned down the Department's reconmendation that a dele-
gation of Japanese unionists be permitted to go to India
1
2
to attend a meeting of the non-Communist Asian Federation
94
of Labor. The stated grounds for his decision were that
the over-all occupation objective of reducing Communist
6
influence in all Japanese labor organizations would be
adversely affected and Conrunists would be furnished with
ammmunition to attack the occupation and advance their
political strength in the unions if non-communist trade
C
unionists were allowed to attend meetings of or affiliate
S
with international labor groups while the same privilege
was withheld fronm Communist-dominated ones. Behind this
C
decision, of course, lay a previously determined position
<
that Comnunist-dominated unions should not be permitted to
E
attend meetings of the World Federation of Trade Unions
(WFTU) or other Communist labor bodies. For apparent
similar reasons the Japanese workers' delegate to the ILo
Coal Mines Committee in Pittsburgh in April (as you know
the ILo is a UN Specialized Agency) was instructed not to
attend in any formal capacity for his union a meeting of
1
the Executive Committee of the United Mine Workers to 
S
which he had been invited by John L. Lewis.
8
6
In my opinion the position taken by SCAP in March,
è
while possibly valid some time ago, is not the best coursa
RET FILE
043
of action at the present time. The Communist-non-Communist
4
struggle
8
SECRET

=== Page 319 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-2-
struggle for power in key Japanese unions can no longer
be regarded as something that can be solved by refusal
to recognize its existence. A deep and basic cleavage
exists today and refusal to recognize this, and give
whatever measure of support we are able to non-Communist
groups, is playing into the hands of the Communist
minorities who have seized power in some unions. Struggles
for control are now taking place within several of the
largest of these Communist-influenced unions, including
those of the railway and communications workers, and,
aided by the dismissal policy of the sovernment in the
current personnel retirement program, there are real
pros ects for suecess on the part of the non-Communist
groups. The argument that the Communists could make
political capital out of public recognition by ScAP of
the existence of a Conumunist-non-Communist division in
the labor movement, and favored treatment for the latter,
is not in my estimation a significant consideration. Full
propaganda use has been and is being made of SCAp's re-
fusal to allow Japanese unionists to attend the recently
held meetings of the USSR trade unions and the WFTU at
Milan, including the charge that he has in fact dis-
criminated by allowing favored union leacers to go abroad
as worlters' representatives to the ILo. More will be made
if he refuses, as exsected, to allow them to attend the
Asian meeting of the MiTU scheduled to be held in Peiping
in November. In brief, whatever we do in this connection
will be used against us, propaganda-wise, hence we should
not let the propaganda factor determine our course of
action to any significant degree.
Advantages that should flow from permitting Japanese
unions to participate in the new world labor organization
are primarily two:
(1) It will counter the considerable upsurge of
commmunist propaganda among labor that will accompany the
WFTU Asian Conference in Hovember and the expected establish-
ment of an Asian egional Office of the organization in
Peiping. as you know, the y"TU announced in February that
it had accepted the membership application of the Liaison
Council of Japanese Trade Unions, and it now lists the nearly
seven million members of the Japanese labor movement in its
own membership. No denial of affiliation has been forth-
coming from the left-wing Council; rather, it sought per-
mission,
SECRET

=== Page 320 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-3-
mission, unsuccessfully, to attend the recent Milan
Conference of the WFTU and will undoubtedly seek to send
delegates to Peiping. Even though permission be refused
it is distinctly possible that some Japanese will turn
up at the Peiping meeting claiming to represent organized
Ja anese labor. The significance of the above stated
considerations lies in the fact that while WFTU ties with
the Japanese unions may be tenuous, and de jure membership
doubtful, both ties and membership status are capable of
satisfactory exploitation within Japan by the Comunists,
and are being so exploited. ention only need be made of
the well-known desire on the part of both the Japanese
people and Japanese organizations for re-admittance into
the international family of nations to understand why
membership in the World Federation of Trade Unions, the
policies and aims of which are not understood by most
Japanese workers or local union leaders, has an undeniably
wide popular appeal. Permission for non-Communist unions
to join the new international will not only satisfy this
desire but will result in a clarification within Japanese
labor circles of what the FTU is and why the recent
split occurred, as well as the wide dissemination of
information concerning the free trade union movement and
its differences from the Soviet type of workers organi-
zation. In my opinion this would be a very salutary
internal development. In my opinion also we must appeal
at this time to the Japanese workers by positive action
rather than by restraints. Increased freedom for Japanese
workers in international contacts is in keeping, I believe,
with United States policy regarding increased inter-
national activities for Japan.
(2) The contacts with free trade union leaders
secured by the Japanese unionists as a result of their
participation in the new organization should prove of
great assistance to them in strengthening their organi-
zations and resisting minority attempts to capture union
control. Benefits in the way of prestige, education in
trade union techniques and how to resist Conmunist tactics
within unions, possible assistance in money and supplies,
etc., will flow from such contacts.
The more significant basis for SCAP's earlier refusal
to permit non-Comminist trade union representatives to
attend meetings of roreign or international worker organi-
zations
SECET

=== Page 321 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-4-
zations appears to be his very reasonable desire not to
allow Communist trade union leaders to attend inter-
national meetings of Communist-controlled workers' organi-
zations, or to have to discriminate openly against them.
While it may be admitted that both of these considerations,
per se, are valid, I believe the importance of broadening
the international contacts of non-Communist Japanese labor
leaders and thereby strengthening them and adding to their
prestige is very much greater.
SCAP could decide, once he agreed that the non-
Communist unions should be allowed to affiliate with
international labor bodies, whether to discriminate and
hence not allow the Communists to attend the WFTU meetings,
for which he could advance very good reasons, or to allow
them to go. While the latter course of action has its
disadvantages, competent observers without exception report
that most intimate contacts exist between extreme leftist
Japanese labor leaders and members of the oversized Soviet
Mission in Tokyo. In view of this situation, any harm to
our interests that could come from allowing Japanese
Communists to go abroad to trade union meetings would fall
almost entirely into prestige and propaganda categories.
Not letting them go, as indicated above, has already
furnished ample opportunity for the latter of these.
Prestige and/or propaganda factors, however, loom small
when contrasted with the fact that on the staff of the
USSR Lission in Tokyo there are undoubtedly highly trained
specialists in how minority groups operate to capture and
utilize for their purposes even unions with predominately
non-Communist memberships, while we do not have a single
United States trade unionist working with Japanese labor
today. Moreover, offers of American trade unions to
send representatives to council with non-Communist Japanese
labor elements in order to give them the benefit of their
experience in resistance to Communist infiltration have
not been accepted by SCAP.
In light of the above I recomnend that in conjunction
with the Departments of Labor and Army the attached wire be
despatched to SCaP informing him of the expected invitation
to Japanese unions from the new organization and recommend-
ing that such qualified non-Communist trade unions as may
be invited to attend be permitted to send representatives.

=== Page 323 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
SCAP,
TOKYO.
Anti-communist trade unions after withdrawing from.
communist-dominated World Federation of Trade Unions met last
June in Geneva, with participation of American trade unions,
to discuss formation of a new anti-communist international
trade union federation. The United States Government has
discreetly encouraged this development and regards it as con-
structive step in current fight against world communism.
Chester Hepler, Chief of Labor Division, who attended ILO
conference held at same time, is fully informed of background
and proceedings of discussions. Preparatory meeting sub-
sequently held in July-August to prepare for founding convention
to convene in London late November. Preparatory Committee,
which includes AFofL and CIO, desires to have Japanese trade
union representation, and we expect invitations to certain
non-Communist Japanese trade unions will shortly be forthcoming.
United States Government has been informally contacted to as-
certain whether representatives of bona fide non-Communist
trade unions,  if invited, will be permitted to attend.
The United States Government believes attendance such
representatives highly desirable for four reasons. First, their
presence at convention will have strong propaganda effect in
Asia in counteracting WFTU claims to represent Asian labor.
This particularly important in light scheduled November meeting
of
SECRET

=== Page 324 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
-2 -
of WFTU Asian unions in Peiping, and expected establishment
of an Asian office. Second, it will be effective step to
foster anti-communist influence among trade unions in Japan.
Third, it will increase the prestige of non-communist unions
and their leadership in Japan. Last, it will encourage unity
of action among the non-communist unions.

=== Page 327 === (confidence 0.99)
dated Se tember 7, 1949 from
the United States Political
Adviser for Japan, Tokyo, on
the sut ject, "Petition from
Ministry of International Trade
and Industry Workers' Labor
Union".
(COPY)
PETITION
August 23, 1949
TO :
The Honorable William J. Sebald
United States Member, Allied Council
or Japan
FROM:
Yoshio Miyamori, Chairman
Ministry of Internatiohal Trade and
Industry Workers' Labdur Union
The program for curtailment in the member of personnel
had been carried on by the Ministry of International Trade
and Industry primarily by means encouraging voluntary
resignation and of recommending submission of the letter
of resignation through promise of making mediation for
obtaining jobs until 15 August when some f its personnel,
whose names are listed in the attached sheet, were advised
to tender their resignation, informed that they would be
dismissed from the service if they would not tender the
letter of resignation by the noon of the following day
and given an official letter of dismissal.
The above mentioned action is none other than a
political action to evict from the Government Offices
communists and present and past officers of the labour
unions, trampling down the Potsdam Declaration, violating
Article 19 of the National Constitution, Article 3 of
the Labour Relation Law, Article 1l of Labour Union Law
and Article 27 of the National Public Service Law, and
serving for an instance manifesting the fascistic character
of the Yoshida Cabinet. If such an action like this is
allowed to be carried out, it is patently clear that
Japan would plunge into a road to war, threatened with
destruction of peace and freedom, resurrection of terroristic
politics of pre-war years and through dumping of commodities
at the expense of low wages and suppression suffered by
the workers。
We ask you in earnest that you would forthwith
start investigation on the affair mentioned above and
would oblige us with your special consideration and attention
for the cancellation of this said unfair action.
Yoshio Miyamori, Chairman,
Ministry of International
Trade and Industry Workers'
Labour Union

SUBJECT: Reported Trade Union Delegate from Japan to National CIO Convention
in the United Stales.
Two Jiji press reports from Tokyo during the course of the past
two weeks have stated that the new non-Communist labor groups are
planning to send a delegate to the CIo convention in the United States.
The last of these reports, dated October 10, states that "Secretary
General EIICHI OCHIAI of the Shin Sanbetsu has been chosen as delagata
Cea
R 894.5043
This Document Mst Be Returned
to the CIo convention in the United States."
that to the best of his knowledge there has been no invitation atended
by the CIo to any Japanese unions for this occasion. He commented
that the CIO did not follow the practice of the AFL and invite large
numbers of fraternal representatives of foreign labor groups to their
convention. I commented that if no invitation had been sent to the
Japanese unions and if the CIo would not welcome a fraternal delegate
from them, it would be wise for us to notify the Labor Division in
SCAP in order to put a halt to any further action on the part of the
Japanese unions in this matter. Ross, who is going to Pittsburgh today
to meet with Secretary-Treasurer Jim Carey, asked that any such step
be deferred until he had consulted with kr. Carey on the matter..
Ross intimated that as far as he knew there would be no objectior to
but that he would like to talk the matter over with Mr. Carey.

Mr. Watson called me this afternoon stating that they had re-
O
ceived information from London that Mr. Vincent Tewson, General
Secretary of the British TUC and Secretary of the Preparatory Com-
1
mittee for the new international organization of free trade unions,
449
had had telegrams from Japan informing him of the organization of
the new Japanese Council for promoting affiliation with the new
body. Tewson reportedly stated that the British TUC welcomes the
formation of this new Japanese group and is in favor of including
representatives from it in the invitation to the London meeting of
C
the New Labor International. He is awaiting further information,
S
however, before cabling the invitation.
B
M
Apparently the messages received by Tewson raised the question
of the Japanese Council's dues to the New Federation. The provisional
regulations of the latter body require payment in advence of attend-
ance at the London meeting, and Tewson was asked whether the Japanese
payments could be deferred until after the meeting. According to
Watson, Tewson stated that this matter could certainly be arranged,
though stressing that he would need some guarantees that the dues
would in time reach the officers of the new body.

With reference to the Mission's tolegram no. 582 of December 22,
1949, in which is quoted General MacARTHUR's reference in a statement
made public today to a letter from the Soviet Member of the Allied
Council for Japan containing charges of oppression by the Japanese
Government, the Acting Political Adviser has the honor to enolose a
copy of the full text of the Soviot letter.
Opening with a quoted subject reading "On anti-democratio measures
of the Japanese Govurnment with rogard to trade unions", the Soviet
lutter proceeds with a recital of a long list of oharges, most of which
have previously been raised in one form or another in the Far Eastern
Commission or other quarters, acousing the Japanese Government of taking
oppressive measures under a "polioy of toleration and indulgence on the
part of the occupation authorities" in the form of brutal police aotion,
mass arrests and iaprisorment of Japanese workers, prohibition of trade
union activities, perscoution of the demooratio press, suppression of
peaceful demonstrations dismissal of progressive professore and teaohers,
and similar "anti-demooratio" measures. Suoh aotion, the lotter states,
"is contrary to the Potsdam Declaration and . . . oreates a real menace
of the revival of the old fasoiat order in Japan." Filled with such
expressions as "bloody supprensions", "pogrom", "police olub-law", "re-
actionary anti-demooratio polioy", "violation of ths Potsdam Deolaration",
and "suppression of fundamental dumooratio freedoms and political rights
of the Japanese people", the letter contains nothing that is new and has
obviously boen produced at this time in order to distract publio atten-
tion from ourrent pressure on the Soviet Union with respect to the prob-
lem of Japanese repatriation. It oonoludes by recommending to the Supreme
Commander that the Japanese Government be required (a) to annul the deci-
sion conoerning the prohibition of politioal activities by government and
other employees, (b) to terminate imediately "the polioe olub-law and
suppression of the activities of trade unions and other demooratio organi-
zations", and (o) to discontinue the practice of making use of the polioy
of dismissals of trade union leaders, professors, and teaohers for polit-
ical ends.
It is not antioipated that the Supreme Comnander will make any reply
FAP
to the Soviet letter beyond that contained in his statement, copy of
whioh is enolosed for ready reference.

GENERAL OF THE ARMY DOUGLAS MACARTHUR
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
Tokyo, Decmmber " ", 1949.
Dear General,
I submit for your consideration the statement and recommendations
on the subject: "On anti-demooratio measures of the Japanese Government
with regard to trade unions".
The Soviet representatives in their statements at the Allied Counoil
for Japan and in the official letters to the Supreme Commander repeatedly
pointed out the numerous faots of gross violations by the Japanese Govern-
ment of the basio democratisation prinoiples of Japan outlined in the
Potsdam Deolaration and in the polioy decisions of the Far Eastern Com-
mission. It is necessary to recall in this connection that according to
the Potsdan Deolaration "the Japanese Government shall remove all obsta-
oles to the revival and strengthening of the domooratio tendenoies among
the Japanese people".
Then the Far Eastern Commission's decision, "the Basio polioy on
Japan after the surrender", says that the Japaness people shall be en-
couraged" to form demooratio and representative organisations".
The Soviet representatives have already drawn the attention of the
Supreme Commander and the Members of the Allied Counoil for Japan to the
inedmissibility of the polioy of toleration and indulgence on the part
of the occupation authorities towards the anti-demooratio ections of the
Japanese Government, the polioy whioh is contrary to the Potsdam Deolara-
tion and whioh oreates a real menace of the revival of the old fascist
order in Japan.
The events of the recent months fully confirm the acouraoy of the
Soviet representatives warnings. This period is sharacterised by further
intensification of the mass police repressions, by new asts of brutal
polioe
UNCLASSIVIED

=== Page 350 === (confidence 0.98)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 895,
December 22, 1949, Enclosure No. 1.
-2
police punishments of the participants of mass demonstrations and
meetings, by mass arrests and imprisonments of hundreds of Japanese
workers, leaders of trade unions and progressive-minded persons, by
the prohibition of lawful activities of trade unions and other publio
organizations, by persecution of the democratic press and the issuance
of new anti-democratic regulations and decrees.
Apart from well knoin facts of bloody suppressions of the peaceful
demonstration near the Tokyo Mumicipality and the strike of the workers
of the Metal plant in Hiroshima, mass unlawful arrests of the workers of
Tokyo Shibaura Electric Company in Kamo and Wakamoto Pharmacy Factory in
Tokyo, to what in due course the Soviet representatives at the Far Eastern
Commission drawnattention, recently there were numerous occasions of new
police repressions.
According to reports of the Kyodo News Agenoy 10 persons were wounded
and ll arrested in Osaka on July the lst during the dispersal of the dem-
onstration of workers representatives numbering 2,000 men, who were meet-
ing the train with repatriates from the USSR. Thirty persons were wounded
in. Kyoto in a similar oase.
According to reports of the same News Agenoy dated July the 17th a
mass rally of the miners of Takahagi (Ibaraki prefecture) who were de-
manding the abolition of the order conoerning unlawful dismissal of 60
workers, was dispersed on July the l6th by the armed Japanese police num-
bering 400 persons. The police arrested 23 workers participating in the
rally.
On Octobur the 2lst this year with the purpose of failing the strike
of workers ut Asano-Uryu coal mines (Hokkaido, Asahigawa region) Japa-
nese police arrested s0 miners who partioipated in t he strike.
The Japanese authorities under the direct support of the occupation
authorities conducted a pogrom campaign against demooratio worker's press
in the middle of August this year, whioh resulted according to the Japa-
nese newspapers, in arrest of l80 leaders and employeus of the worker's
press and progressive public men same of whom were brought to military
trial and montenoed to hard labor. A number of Japanese publio men in
the field of oulture and eduoation was also brought to the Military
trial and sentenoed to hard labor for the partioipation in the demonstra-
tion in Urave town against the prohibition of politioal activities of
teaohers and students.
It is well known fast that by the order of Japanese authorities
the demooratio Korean organimations - the League of Korean Residents in
Japan and the Korean Youth Demooratic Federation whioh unite more than
500 thousand of Koreans, were amashed by Japanese police on Septumber.
the 9th and all the property of these organisations was confiscated and
more than 300 progressive Korean workers arrested.
In order to justify the police olub-law and mass arrests the Japa-
nese authorities orgunised recently a number of provocative trials
arranging them with various precaution police measures against general
publioity (admission of seleoted visitors to the trials only with speciel
permissions, prohibition to make publio the progress of the trial in the
press and so on).
Such is
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 351 === (confidence 0.98)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 895;
December 22, 1949, Enclosure No. 1.
-3.-
Such is far from being complete list of facts of police repres-
sions in Japan which became widely known.
The Japanese Government openly uses mass diamissals in state enter-
prises and offices in order to discharge from the enterprises and offices
progressive leaders of trade unions and disagreeable persons, to split
and smash trade unions. For instance; the progressive leadership of
Railway Workers Union, Communication Workers Union, Agricultural Ministry
workers Union and other state employees unions was diamissed under the
pretext of staff-reduction. General Walker, Commander of the 8th Occupa-
tion Army in Japan in his statement on September the 16th virtually con-
firmed that the Japanese Government makes use of mass dismissals for
political purposes.
After the appeal of Mister Eells, SCAP representative, at the
university opening ceremony in Niigata on July the 19th this year the
"purge" and dismissals of progressive professors and teachers from
universities began all over tho country.
According to information of the Japanese press dozens of prominent
representatives of science were dismissed from thoir service up to this
tims and in this way is conducted an unmasked "police thought control"
formerly praotised by the Japanese militarists.
All this leads to the sigmificant inorease of a number of persons
who are discontented with the police olub-law, lawlessness and violence
established in Japan.
With the intention to make the Japanese legislation fit reactionary
anti-democratic polioy the Japanese.authorities arbitrary change the
existing legislation, issue new obviously anti-demooratio regulations,
provisions and decrees and plan to issue new reactionary laws.
Thus, on August the 12th the government agenoius issued the ciroular
about new rules of the registration of political organizations whioh vir-
tually establishes government control over these orgunimations. All
Japan Liaison Trade Union Council and League for Dofonce of Demooracy,
whioh unite in their ranks 15 millions of organized Japanese, in their
offioial protests to Yoshida Government, rightly oharaoterised this
measure as rude violation of the rights of Japanese people for partioipa-
tion in political activities.
The Japanese Cabinet unlawfully interfering in the internal activities
of trade unions and violating formerly existed provisions on August the
12th took' the docision according to whioh all dismissed workers and em-
ployeos are mechanically deprived of their right to be trade union members
and that all dismissed leaders of trade unions have no right to represent
the interests of their voters. The protests on the part of trade unions
and the publio against this unlawful decision are boing continued up to
this date.
According to the materials published by the Japanese press in the
end of August this year the Jurisdiotion Comaission of the House of
Councillors is working out a bill to prosecute political and publio
organizations and their leaders for different "unlawful" aotions committed
by any of their members.
The bill
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 352 === (confidence 0.98)
Tokyo's Despatoh No. 895,
December 22, 1949, Enclosure No. a.
-4 -
The bill provides hard labor up to 10 years for the participation
in "collective acts of violence" what may be easily applied to any
labor dispute, damonstration and rally. As it is known the announce-
ment about the preparation of this bill caused a great indignation of
the Japanese ublic and press. Even the rightest Japanese newspaper
could not but point out that this bill in its contents "is even worse
than the old "Thought police Law" ("Asahi", August the 26th).
"It reminds us, writes "Asahi", old chinese law by which all the
relatives of the family of a guilty person up to 9 generation bore the
responsibility for some fault".
The same aim to fight the democratic movement has the bill on
forced dactylography registration of the workers and employees which is
being worked out by the government.
According to "Kyodo" report dated September the lOth, this dactylog-
raphy registration is already carried out in Saitama prefocture.
Enjoying the full freedom from punishment and having the evident
support of the Occupation Authorities, the Japanese Government dared to
a new unprecedented reactionary measure depriving the employees and
workers of the Government and public service of olementary political
rights. On September, the 17th, the National Personnel Authority Board
of the Cabinet published the decision whioh forbids all the Government
and State aployees, nwabering more than one million members of the trade
unions, to support and criticime the political parties and organizations,
to be the leadere of thuse organizations, to oriticize the existing Cabi-
net, to demand the dissolution of any organizations or the discharge of
any Governnent officials, to take part in the political campaigns, to
organize demonstrations and rallies, to publish or distribute printed
party organs, to muke speeches or to uxpress their politioal views, to
writo books or artiolos on political subjects and so on. For the viola-
tion of all above mentioned provisins 3 years of imprisonment and fine
to 100,000 yen is provided.
Thin is completely open and demonstrative violation of the Potsdam
Deolaration, whioh provides that in Japan "shall be established the free-
dom of speeoh, of religion and of thought as well as rempect for the
fundamental human rights" and the violation of the artiole 6 of the Far
Easturn Comission's decision on "The principles for Japanese Trade Unione"
proclaiming that "Trade Unions should be allowed to take part in politioal
activities and to support political parties". All thio caused the deep
indignation of all groups of the Japanese population. Almost all politi-
cal parties, trade unions and publio organimations on behalf of millions
of their members made official protests against this decision, desoribing
it as a rude violation of the rights of the Japanese people for political
activities and demanding its abolition.
Many trade unions ment the petitiona to the Soviet Reprementative
in Japan in whioh this Government measure is qualified am "a complete
suppression of basic human rights and trade union activities", and as
leading to "the revival of militant fasoism" (from the petition of the
All Japan Liaison Trade Unions Counoil, representing four million mombera).
Those steps
UNCLASSIPIHD

=== Page 353 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 895,
December 22, 1949, Enclosure No. 1.
-5-
These steps of the Japanese Government and the American Occupation
Authorities, without knowledge of which these measures could not be con-
ducted, are directed at the present time not to the carrying out of the
democratization of Japan in conformity with the Potsdam Declaration and
with the Political Decisions of the Far Eastern Commission but they pur-
sue the purposes of suppression of fundamental democratic freedoms and
political rights of the Japanese people, which directly leads to the
revival of the fascist order existed in Japan before the capitulation.
The Supreme Commander cannot ignore these direct violations of the adopted
decisions by the Allied Powers.
In view of rude violation of the Potsdam Declaration and the decision
of the Far Eastern Commission on the democratization of Japan by the Japa-
nese Government and because of conducting by it anti-democratic measures
accompanied by the police club-law, lawlesness and violence I submit for
the consideration of the Supreme Commander the following recommendations:
1) To demand from the Japanese Jovernment the immediate abolition of
the decision concerning the prohibition of the political activities for
the Government and state employees as well as the revision by the Japa-
nese Diet of the existing Publio Service Law as the Law violating the
basic principles of the Potsdam Declaration and the politioal decisions
of the Far Eastern Conmission. On the same ground to demand the abolish-
ment of the accepted by the Government Agencies anti-demooratic provi-
sions as well as discontinuation of the preparation of the above-men-
tioned new anti-denooratio legislations.
2) To demand from the Japanese Government to stop immediately the
police club-law and suppression of the aotivities of trade unions and
other demooratic organizations as well as repressions against the leaders
of the trado unions and other demooratio publio men and to demand from the
Government to punish those who are responsible for oarrying mass arrests.
3) To suggest to the Japanese Govertument to stop immediately the
practice of making use for the politioal ends of the dismissals of the
trade union leaders and other publio organisations as well as professors
and teachers, and to reinstate at the work all unlawfully discharged
persons.
Sinoorely yours,
/ K. Derevyanko
LIEUTENANT-GENERAL
K.DEREVYANKO

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
FAR EAST COMMAND
Public Information Office
22 December 1949
Immediate Release:
GENERAL MACARTHUR ACCEPTS COUNCIL RECOIMENDATION
General MacArthur has just issued the following statement:
I have accepted the wise recommendation of the British Common-
wealth Member made at yesterday's meeting of the Allied Council to
seek the help of a neutral nation such as Switzerland, or of the
International Red Cross, to gather the fullest possible details of
the tragio fate of the 376,000 missing Japanese primoners. To this
end I am requesting the United States Government to attempt to ne-
gotiate the necessary arrangements. I oan well understand the re-
luctance of the Soviet Member yesterday to listen to so gruesome
and savage a story in all its harrowing barbarity. It could well
chill and sioken even a hardened old soldier.
The Soviet Hember has now given to the press a letter con-
taining charges of oppression by the Japanese Covernmont. These
moth-eaton ohargen huve been so often male by the Soviets and so
often irrefutably shown to be completely prejudiced, if not aotu-
ally false, that their continued repetition could well be iunored
under normal cirounstances as merely blatant propaganda. But when,
as at this moment, they are ugain taken from the shelf and freshly
dusted off to aot as a wmoke soreen to distraot attention from the
investigation of the dreadful fate of hundreds of theusande of
Japanese prisoners probably dead on Soviet soil, they ropresent a
callousnoss of hypoorisy I oannot fail to denounce.
